 THE DOUBLE THIRTEEN




 PART I




 CHAPTER I.
 HON. ROBERT BARLING

“My dear Fleury, if life held nothing except this roast chicken,
life would be worth living.”

The head waiter of the Monopole Hotel bowed.

“I am glad that _m’sieur_ is satisfied. That signifies much; is it not
so? But the roast chicken”--a smile flickered on his lips--“it is not
so simple as it looks.”

But the young man whom he served withered that enthusiasm with a
glance which declared that he had extended his interest in the matter
to its utmost limits.

The head waiter retired, a little crestfallen, perhaps, but still well
pleased with the compliment he had received. Was not the Hon. Robert
Barling the most distinguished of all those whom it is worth while
serving with the very best?

True, a young man of melancholy aspect, little given to
familiarity--but with taste, discretion, knowledge. He signed to the
waiter whom he had appointed to attend the distinguished visitor and
whispered some new directions in his ear.

The dinner proceeded in exquisite leisure. The Hon. Bob tasted a
cutlet which was scarcely less what it seemed to be than the chicken
he had approved. The wine waiter brought a bottle, lying snugly in its
cradle, and reverently refilled his glass with the magnificent
Mantrachet which he specially favored. The noble wine gathered points
of golden light to its bosom.

He sipped it as though he scarcely realized what he was doing. His
eyes were curiously vacant. Nevertheless, they wandered from table to
table of the huge dining room as though, unconsciously, he was looking
for some one. Even when the last of the courses had been served and he
was sipping his coffee, that search continued. He sighed deeply. Then
he took a golden cigarette case from his pocket and lit a very thin
cigarette of a type unknown to the open market.

He was a tall young man, of excessively restrained manner. His friends
said of him that he was starving himself to death because the world
contained nothing good enough for him to eat. They said also that he
had already driven half the tailors in Saville Row into lunatic
asylums, because they could not live up to his ideas of a perfectly
fitting coat or a waistcoat cut without a flaw.

And they added that his valet had been selected out of ten thousand
candidates. But most of them did him the compliment of slavish
imitation, even to the weary expression which, of late months, his
eyes had begun to assume.

These eyes were decidedly interesting. They were gray, and rather
large, and the lids drooped on them in a manner which challenged
curiosity. Women especially always found themselves wondering what
secret sorrow this very rich, very popular young man was nursing in
his heart. There were men, though, tough, rough men who had followed
Bob Barling into action in France and who knew that the gray eyes
could smile in the face of death.

His car was waiting for him at the door of the hotel. He told his
driver to take him to Brook Street, and the man did not ask him for
the number. He lay back on the cushions as though a sudden fatigue had
overwhelmed him. The big _Hispano Suiza_ glided into the traffic of
the Haymarket like a great cat. A few minutes later it came to the
curb of its destination. A commissionaire opened the door, and the
Hon. Bob passed under the awning which had been erected in front of
the house.

He was early, he whose rule it was always to be late. He greeted his
host and hostess on the stairs and walked into the ballroom. The gray
eyes opened wide for a moment and their glance was eager and
expectant, but the next instant they had drooped again.

He made his way to one of the windows and stood there as though he had
posted himself at a vantage point. It seemed that he knew very few of
his fellow guests. His face was quite impassive, but the corners of
his mouth twitched occasionally in a nervous fashion.

The band played a fox trot and the floor became crowded. He drew back
a little to avoid the dancers. Several girls glanced at him as they
passed--envious glances, for the most part. But he noticed nothing.
Always his eyes were fixed on the doorway.

And then, suddenly, he started. His tall, lean figure seemed to
stiffen. The indifferent expression faded from his face. A close
observer would have noticed that his hands were tightly clenched, as
though he were making a supreme effort to master himself.

The cause of this change in Bob Barling’s attitude was the entry of a
young girl, with a pale, rather melancholy face. She was not, at a
first glance, very striking-looking, but there was a quality in her
expression which challenged attention--a curious, aloof quality, as
though her thoughts were far away from the world in which she lived.

The young man made his way round the room to her side. As he
approached she raised her eyes and saw him. For a moment a look of
anxiety crossed her face.

“You see, I await you,” he whispered.

“Oh, yes--always.”

She began to dance with him. They went round the room without
speaking. It seemed that the far-away look in her eyes faded a little
as she gave herself to the rhythm of the music, as though, in dancing,
she had found again a little of that inward self which at other
moments was so imperfectly realized.

“My mother is not feeling very well tonight,” she told him. “She will
not come into the ballroom.”

He said that he was sorry. His voice seemed strangely excited, so that
she glanced up into his face. Their eyes met.

“My God, Olva, how beautiful you are!”

It was tremendously unlike him--the tone, the manner, the thrill which
made the words a confession of uncontrollable longing.

For an instant she seemed to relinquish her self-control.

“Oh, please,” she whispered, “you know you promised.”

“But I shall break my promise. I have come here to break it.” He added
in tones which were defiant. “I have come here because that promise
must be broken.”

He led her out of the room and they sat down behind some palms which
partially screened them from their fellow-guests. He leaned forward
toward her with his elbows set on his knees and his chin in his hands.

“When I made that promise,” he declared, “I didn’t know how serious it
was. I didn’t realize that the world holds nothing for me except you.”

Olva Vorloff closed her eyes. Her lips trembled, but she remained
silent, as though she were debating with herself, feverishly, what she
should do with him.

“Last night I didn’t sleep at all. I have scarcely slept since I saw
you. I think I know what it feels like to go mad.”

“But, dear, if I do not love you.”

“I shall make you love me.”

She opened her eyes widely and looked up in his face.

“Will you?”

There was not a trace of resentment in her tones. For an instant she
seemed to have put away the shadowy side of her nature and to have
become merely a young girl approaching timidly to her happiness. He
put out his hands and laid them on hers.

“Even if you said that you hated me, I should not be discouraged.
Olva, I love you so completely that I scarcely care at all whether or
not you love me in return. If you do not come to me of your own free
will I will take you.”

As he spoke his face assumed a stern expression, which made him seem
older and harder. It was scarcely possible to recognize the exquisite
youth who had dined so languidly an hour before.

“How will you take me?” she asked.

He looked into her big, clear eyes and smiled in spite of himself.
There was a childlike quality about this Russian girl which he had
never experienced in any other women. An English girl who had suffered
as she had suffered and lost as she had lost would have been changed
in that terrible process.

“I don’t know yet. And, anyhow, it will not be necessary, because I
know that you love me.” Again his voice had fallen to a whisper. “Is
it not so?”

She bowed her head, but he caught the word “Yes,” spoken very faintly
with just a trace of a foreign accent.

His hand tightened on her hand.

“Then we are engaged, because I know that Countess Vorloff will not
refuse me. We must go and find her.”

“No, please.” Her eyes were beseeching. “I want to talk to you first.”

She leaned forward in her chair and looked at him so intently that
even _he_ felt a sense of embarrassment. It seemed as if she would
read his soul.

“In Russia,” she declared, “we say of love that it transforms the
spirit. Those who love are changed beyond recognition.”

“I believe that.”

“They have new strength--so that nothing is able to harm them any
more.”

“Yes.”

Again her eyes drooped. He looked at her and felt himself thrilled by
the divine quality of her beauty. So, he thought, it must have shone
in her girlhood days, when the splendid world of St. Petersburg waited
to do her homage and life seemed to have denied her nothing of
happiness or comfort.

Since then, the bitterness of death had been her portion, insult,
cruelty, privation, the long terror of imprisonment and the horror of
flight. If only it had been granted to him to stand beside her during
that ordeal.

“My darling,” he whispered, “nothing shall harm you any more. I swear
it!”

She glanced up, and then, suddenly, he saw dawn in her eyes a look of
such extreme fear as he had never before witnessed in a human face.
She clutched his arm with hands from which the blood seemed to have
ebbed away.

Instinctively he glanced behind him.

There was no one in sight.




 CHAPTER II.
 THE FACE AT THE WINDOW

He did his best to comfort her, and after a few moments she regained
her self-control.

“I am haunted--haunted,” she cried. “When I least expect them the
spectres of the past rise up before my eyes. That day when they
dragged my father from us and we knew that we should not see him
again.” She stopped suddenly and glanced wildly about her. “Oh,
quickly, take me away from here--home. I must not stay here. Do you
understand?”

He conducted her to her mother, bowing ceremoniously before that lady,
whose eyes seemed so dim toward the world.

Olva had recovered herself somewhat, but her face was still dreadfully
pale, and he observed that she continued to tremble. He had seen a
child behave in that way after it had been frightened, a small niece
of his own, whose dry sobbing had actually for a time punctuated her
laughter, as though forgetfulness penetrated but slowly to the deeper
places of the spirit.

“Olva says that she is tired,” he explained, “and that you are not
feeling well. May I beg the honor of taking you home? My car is
waiting.”

Countess Vorloff glanced from his anxious face to that of her
daughter. She nodded.

“It is so kind,” she said enigmatically.

She was an old woman, yet the beauty of her girlhood had not wholly
faded; nor the simplicity of her manner, which must, when she was
young, have been a great charm. Bob thought that in this respect
mother and daughter were strangely alike. Or perhaps it was the race
to which they belonged. With its faults, the aristocracy of old Russia
was exquisitely simple and straightforward; “an aristocracy of
children,” as one of its closest observers has written of it.

He told a footman to call his car, and a moment later they were
rolling through the streets. Olva seemed to enjoy that gliding motion,
for she smiled without speaking. But her mother was voluble. As
always, she kept harking back to the days of her glory in the
Petersburg of the eighties. It had become an obsession with her.

“So it was, through streets like these, but to the music of the sleigh
bells. And now there is death--death. Ah, _mon Dieu_, how good was
life in those days.” Her voice trailed away and she sighed deeply.
Then a new flood of memory swept her spirit.

“Olva’s father, my poor, dear husband, had official duties at the
Winter Palace, which often kept him there till a late hour. And
sometimes I would call for him on my way home from a ball or party and
we would drive back together across the crisp snow of the Prospect,
which was jeweled like a woman’s corsage.”

The car slackened pace and stopped at number ten South Street. Bob
alighted and handed out the Countess. Then he gave his hand to Olva.
She bent to him and whispered:

“I shall not ask you to come in tonight. I am very tired, you see.”

She followed her mother quickly across the pavement and disappeared
into the house. On the threshold she turned her head for an instant
and Bob saw that she was still smiling. He lingered until the door was
shut, and then told his man to take him to the Mayfair Club.

He threw himself back on the cushions of the car in great confusion
and perplexity of mind. The happiness which he should have felt was
tempered by a nameless anxiety, almost a fear. Why had Olva consented
so reluctantly to his wooing, seeing that, as she had confessed, she
loved him? And what apparition of terror was it which had risen
suddenly in her eyes? At such a moment it was doubly hard to be
compelled to leave her, when his whole nature desired ardently to
bring her protection and comfort.

And yet, he told himself a moment later, her word was easily
comprehensible when the fact of her bitter sufferings was taken into
account. Her father had been one of the first to perish at the hands
of the Bolshevists. She and her mother had spent nearly a year in the
Peter and Paul fortress, and had only made their escape because they
happened to possess a friend whose power was great among the new
rulers of Russia.

The countess had dropped a hint that the services of this unknown had
not been entirely disinterested. Happily, her prudent husband had
invested considerably in British Funds during the early days of the
war.

He sent his car away when he reached the club. The distance to his
house in Curzon street was nothing, and, in any case, he liked walking
in London after midnight. He gave his hat and gloves to the man at the
door and strolled into a big, exotically decorated room, where half a
dozen young men and girls were dancing in a curiously languid fashion.

He sat down at one of the small tables which flanked the walls and
ordered a whiskey and soda. A moment later the music stopped and one
of the girls came across the floor to him.

“Why, you great stranger!” she exclaimed. “Where have you been all
this time?” She sat down and waved her late partner away. “But I know,
of course--following in the footsteps of the lovely Olva _n’est ce
pas_? I saw her this very afternoon in Bond Street.”

“My dear Beryl, you see everything, of course.”

“Not everything! But what I do not see, I guess; and that is better
still, as a rule.”

Bob glanced at her pretty, naughty face. Beryl Lestrange belonged to
pleasure as the stars belong to the night--one of the multitude of
rich girls who rather mysteriously seem to suffer no restrictions on
their complete freedom of action. Of her antecedents he knew nothing
at all.

“Jack Derwent brought me along from the Wincomes,” she told him.
“Those dear people, you know, who have taken Windermere House. It’s at
least three sizes too big for them. All sorts of people there--kind of
human zoo, with the emphasis on the Small Cats’ House.”

The rather weary violin began to play again and they got up and
danced.

“The Countess Olva had a very handsome man shopping with her this
afternoon,” she said. “A big, tall man, with the kind of eyes which
women remember.” She raised her shoulders in a gesture of mock terror.
“You know--which look through you to the horizon.”

Bob did not answer her. She glanced up and saw that, in some way, she
had vexed him. Was he so jealous of the girl that he could not bear to
hear of a possible rival? A little shudder of mischief ran along her
nerves. She tossed her beautiful golden head impatiently.

“Always,” she declared, “when I have found a man’s eyes thrill me, I
have found also that that man had foreign blood in his veins, just as
I have myself. Englishmen lack the power to make a woman’s head go
dizzy. A pity, is it not?”

“Possibly, if one covets dizziness.”

His voice was hard. She felt sorry, suddenly, that she had teased him.
She changed the subject, bidding him observe that the club was
beginning to fill up.

“One wonders where they all come from--and at the same hour. It is
possible to be so regular in irregularity. The next stage to night
clubs, believe me, is the nursery, where at least boisterous innocence
is unequivocal.”

He took her back to their table. The troubled look was big in his eyes
now. He did not even so much as glance at the people who greeted him
as they passed into the room.

“Tell me,” he asked in low tones, “what the man you saw with the
Countess Olva was really like.”

His voice was pleading, as though he awaited her answer in great
anxiety.

“Tall--I said that, didn’t I? With a big forehead and a big chin. And
then those queer eyes of his.”

Bob bit his lips.

“There was nothing else--nothing special about his appearance?”

She thought a moment.

“Why, yes,” she declared. “He had a scar on his left cheek. I
distinctly remember noticing it and wondering if he had got it in a
duel.”

The young man’s expression changed. He rose abruptly.

“Thank you,” he said.

“What, you aren’t going yet? At one o’clock!”

“I am tired tonight, you see.”

He strode out of the room, an easy, athletic figure. Beryl followed
him with her eyes till he disappeared behind the group of palms in the
entrance hall.

He walked toward Piccadilly, and when he reached it turned in the
direction of Hyde Park Corner. The thoughts which tormented him
quickened his steps. He came to the top of St. James’s Street and
paused for a moment to allow a cab to go by. Then he strode on past
the Ritz to the railings of the Green Park.

He paused there and stood a moment irresolute. What Beryl Lestrange
had told him filled his mind now, to the exclusion of every impression
coming from the outside world. Her idle tongue had forced on him an
anxiety he would have given anything to have escaped.

Just after Olva had implored him to take her away from the dance they
had passed a man, obviously a foreigner, with a deep scar on his left
cheek.

Olva, so far as he could recollect, had shown no sign of recognition
and yet at the time he had experienced a curious feeling that they
knew each other. That was what had prompted him to ask Beryl to
describe the fellow.

Possibly the fact that the man’s eyes had searched his own face so
intently had given him that feeling. Beryl was quite right in what she
had said about the compelling quality of his eyes. But why had these
two disowned their acquaintance in his presence? And could there be
any connection between the fear the girl had shown so suddenly and the
coming of this mysterious stranger?

He tried to recollect the scene in its minute details and realized at
once that the man, whoever he was, might have been watching them from
the door of the ballroom as they sat together. In that case he would
be facing Olva the whole time. Yet a single step would bring him
inside the doorway, and so place him out of sight.

He began to walk briskly again as his perplexity increased. It was
surely incredible that a friend with whom one went shopping in the
afternoon should have become an object of extreme terror by nightfall.
Yet that was the conclusion to which he seemed to be coming, for he
felt no manner of doubt that Beryl had told him the truth.

He crossed Piccadilly and entered the bottom of Park Lane. Was it
possible that this man was connected in some way with the terrible
experiences in Russia which Olva and her mother had undergone?

If so, his sudden appearance when she did not expect to see him might
serve to open the sealed doors of memory. That would just barely
account for her agony of apprehension. He told himself that it would
not furnish even a bare explanation of her lack of recognition. Unless
indeed--.

He stopped again in sudden relief, as the idea took shape in his mind.
Olva had walked with her eyes downcast on the floor. He distinctly
remembered noticing that. It was just possible that she might have
passed the stranger without seeing him at all.

He observed a cab approaching and signaled to the driver to stop. Then
he noticed that the flag was lowered. He was about to resume his walk
when an exclamation of amazement broke from his lips.

The light of the street lamp had fallen on the window of the vehicle,
revealing a woman’s face.

_It was Olva._

He sprang forward, but the cab was traveling quickly. A moment later
he saw the tail-lamp disappear round the bend into Piccadilly. A
private car followed. He ran down the street after it, searching in
vain for some means by which to carry on the pursuit, but there was no
cab in sight. At the corner of Park Lane he stood, out of breath,
viewing the wide space of Hyde Park Corner, which appeared for the
moment to be utterly deserted.




 CHAPTER III.
 A MIDNIGHT VISIT

Bob stood irresolute. Had the cab gone down toward Victoria, or had
it turned into Knightsbridge? He ran along to the gates of the Park
and scanned both roadways. As it happened, they were both empty at the
moment. A sense of chill despair gripped his heart.

He had no doubt whatever that the girl whom he had seen in the cab was
Olva. The street lamp had given him a full view of her face, though
she had certainly not observed him. How pale she had looked, how
anxious, as though she had set her mind to the accomplishment of some
terrible duty!

A cab swung into sight from Park Lane. He hailed it, and got into it.

“Drive along Knightsbridge,” he told the man, “as fast as you can.”

They lurched away. Bob sat at the window scanning the street in front
of him and craning his neck to search every side street they passed.
They came to the Albert Hall, and he ordered the driver to turn down
into Kensington. A few seconds later they reached the Cromwell Road.
Bob knocked on the glass.

“Turn back to Curzon Street, number fifty--drive slowly.”

He flung himself back on the hard leather cushions. He had known from
the beginning that the quest was hopeless, but his restless nerves had
not allowed him to relinquish it until the last feeble chance was
exhausted. Now, in the reaction of feeling which followed his failure,
he experienced a sense of depression greater than he had ever known
before. He closed his eyes, scarcely daring to think. Yet his thoughts
rushed on in spite of him.

It was certain that, at this hour of the morning, the girl he loved
could not be going on any ordinary errand. Had she not told him she
was tired and meant to get to bed early? She had abstained, too, from
asking him into the house. Thus something must have
happened--something unexpected and terrible, to make her change her
plans and go out again, alone, on her mysterious mission.

He tortured his brain to imagine what this emergency might be. Then a
darker thought came to haunt him. Was it possible that Olva had
planned this midnight expedition beforehand? That would explain why
she had wished to return home early. It would explain, too, her
reluctance to sit up and talk to him. It might even explain the
strange episode of the man with the scarred face.

He opened his eyes. The cab was passing the High Court Hotel. He
sprang forward and shouted to the driver to stop.

As the vehicle came to the curb he turned and looked out of the rear
window. Then he leaned back stiffly on his seat. A man, dressed in a
long, foreign-looking cloak and slouch hat, came abreast of the cab
and stood a moment as though waiting for its occupant to alight.

Bob bent down sharply, cursing himself at the same time for being a
fool. When he looked up again the man walked some distance away.

He opened the offside window and told the driver to follow, no matter
where he went. He had caught a glimpse of the fellow as he passed
under one of the street lamps. It was the man with the scarred face.

That individual walked very slowly and looked round several times.
Then he turned up the collar of his cloak and drew his hat down. It
was obvious that he was anxious to avoid recognition.

Bob sat back watching his every movement. Beryl’s description of his
tall figure tallied exactly. His carriage, too, stiffer than that of
an Englishman, was quite characteristic. He looked, it had to be
admitted, exceedingly distinguished.

A cab passed, going slowly toward Piccadilly. The stranger hailed it
and got in. The vehicle continued on its way to Hyde Park Corner and
turned left-handed toward Park Lane. A moment later it had swung to
the right toward Park Street.

Bob caught his breath. Was it possible that the fellow was going to
the Vorloff’s house in South Street? He had not long to wait for
assurance on that point. Almost before his own driver realized it the
cab they were following had drawn up at number ten. They drove on past
it and stopped on the opposite side of the street in front of a block
of flats.

Bob saw the stranger get out and ascend the steps. To his amazement,
the door was opened at once and the man passed inside. But the cab
awaited him. Then, after about ten minutes, he reappeared on the
doorstep. He ran down the steps and reëntered the vehicle, which at
once swung round and disappeared in the direction of the Park.

“Follow it!”

They sighted their quarry at Hyde Park Corner. It returned to the High
Court Hotel. The stranger paid off the cabman there and strode into
the building. Bob’s driver turned to receive fresh orders.

“You had better take me to Curzon Street now.”

For a moment, in the excitement of the pursuit, the young man had
almost forgotten his anxiety. But now it returned to him a
hundredfold. What could be the meaning of these mysterious doings? He
could not doubt that Olva must have returned home in time to receive
her visitor, since it was incredible that the old Countess had
remained up till that hour.

Her health, as he knew, was exceedingly precarious. Nor could he doubt
that the visit had been prearranged. No one, least of all a man such
as this, would dream of rousing a household at that hour without
having been invited beforehand to do so.

It followed that the woman he loved had gone shopping with the man in
the morning; had shown extreme fear at the sight of him in the
evening--so much so that she had passed him without a sign of
recognition, after having begged to be taken home at once--and then
had received him secretly in the small hours of the morning at her own
home.

Further, before receiving him, she had gone away on some mysterious
midnight errand. The mystery, he realized, was too deep to be solved
by any piecing together of the scraps of evidence which Fortune and
his own anxiety had afforded him. Fear and horror descended on his
spirit.

He paid the cabman and let himself into the house with his latchkey.
He went immediately to his smoking-room. It was decorated in the deep
tones of red--the russets of autumn--in which his heart specially
delighted. Every detail expressed his fastidious, yet easy taste. It
expressed also the strength of character which underlay his _savoir
faire_. He poured himself out a whisky and soda and drank it at a
gulp.

Then he lit one of his thin cigarettes--discarding it a moment later
for an Abdullah, which seemed to offer the soothing his fevered nerves
demanded. He sank down into one of his great armchairs and sat gazing
in front of him, attempting once again to read the terrible riddle
which had become now the riddle of his own fate.

He fell asleep at that task. He was awakened by the sharp ringing of
the telephone bell in the room. He started to his feet. The gray light
of an October morning was already in the sky. He strode across the
room and took the instrument from its stand.

“Yes, this is Mr. Barling’s house. Mr. Barling speaking. Who is that?
What! Countess Vorloff.”

His hands tightened on the instrument so that the blood was driven
from his knuckles. The Countess’s voice, always a trifle guttural,
came to him thickly on the wires. She was telling him that Olva had
gone out during the night and had not returned, and asking him,
clearly in an agony of apprehension, if, as her most intimate friend,
he could throw any light on the mystery.

“But she returned before two o’clock, surely,” he cried. “I saw a
visitor myself call at that hour.”

“No, no! It is not so! She has not come.”

“I assure you the man was admitted to the house.”

In his anxiety he did not care whether or not she knew of his doings
the night before.

“Ah yes, I know. There was a letter for him which she left with the
servant. But herself is gone. _Mon Dieu_, it is terrible, is it not?”

“You mean you did not know she was going out?”

“Nothing. Nothing.”

The voice thrilled with fear. The horrors of the revolutionary days in
Petersburg had left their indelible mark.

Bob glanced at his watch. It was a quarter to six. He promised to come
around immediately to South Street.




 CHAPTER IV.
 MR. DANATOFF’S APPOINTMENT

The Countess Vorloff’s fears were so acute and her nerves so cruelly
shaken that it took Bob a considerable time to discover exactly what
had happened on the previous evening. At last, however, he ascertained
that as soon as Olva and her mother entered the house the former went
to the library to write a letter. She remained there till the old lady
had gone to bed.

Then she summoned their manservant and gave him a closed envelope,
unaddressed, which she told him he was to hand to a man who would call
about two A.M. On no account was the letter to leave his possession
until then; he was not even to lay it on the hall table.

The manservant noticed that the letter had been carefully sealed with
the purple wax which the Countess Olva always affected. After she had
given these instructions Olva went to her room. About half an hour
later she appeared in the hall wearing outdoor clothes, and told the
man to call a cab immediately. He did not hear the instructions which
she gave to the driver.

They were seated in the Countess’s boudoir, where a fire had already
been lighted.

The servant was summoned at Bob’s request. He was a Russian, named
Peter Nikolaiovitch, who had lived for many years in England. He
corroborated the story in every detail.

“Was the Countess Olva quite calm, did you notice, when she gave you
these orders?” the young man asked.

“She was perfectly calm, sir. But she was very pale in the face--much
paler than usual.”

“She did not strike you as being afraid of--of anything?”

“I thought she seemed anxious.”

Bob paused a moment and knit his brow.

“Did the gentleman who called for the letter give you his name?”

“No, sir.”

“Did you ask him to give it?”

“No, sir. Her ladyship told me not to ask any questions.”

“I see.”

Bob thought a moment.

“You have not seen this visitor before?”

“Yes, sir; I have seen him before, but I think only twice.” The man
seemed to hesitate. “He called earlier in the evening when I was out.
Her ladyship’s maid opened the door to him.”

“Ah!” Bob drew his breath sharply. “Do you happen to know if the maid
told him where her mistress was to be found?”

“Yes, sir. She told him their ladyships had gone to the dance in Brook
Street.”

Bob drew a deep breath. He glanced at the Countess, but now she seemed
to have become indifferent to what was passing. Her old eyes looked
out across the terrible vista of the years, seeing only ruin and fear.
It was obvious that already she had imagined some new, terrible
catastrophe as the cause of her daughter’s disappearance.

“The gentleman had a scar on his cheek? Am I right?”

“Yes, sir; on his left cheek.”

The young man considered a moment.

“Her ladyship,” he asked, “goes out occasionally at night?”

“Yes, sir.”

Again he glanced at the Countess. The vacant look in her eyes had not
changed. He signed to the man that he might retire.

“Will you tell me,” he asked the old lady, “why you should be so
anxious this time, seeing that your daughter has gone away before in
the same manner and at the same hour?”

Countess Vorloff started. She must have been listening more carefully
than was apparent, because she answered at once.

“Olva left a note for me, as well as for her visitor. I could not
sleep and rang the bell for some medicine I take. My maid found the
note under the door of my bedroom.”

“May I see it?”

She handed him a crumpled sheet of paper which she had been clutching
in her hand. He spread it out. The letter was written in French, the
language mother and daughter used in familiar conversation. He read:


 My Dear Mother:

 I must go away to-night. I may not be able to return home for some
 time. Do not worry about me, and please do not try to find me. I shall
 be quite safe. I dare not say more at present.

                                                          Olva.

 P.S.--Please, above all things, do not call in the police, however
 long I am absent from home.


He looked up with a new fear in his eyes.

“You have no idea where--where she goes to on these occasions?”

“None, none. Before I have supposed it was to dance. In London now it
is the fashion to turn night into day--for young people. And Olva will
not permit that I interfere in her life. Ah, _mon Dieu_, if only I had
been firm with her as I was with her poor, dear father. But when she
was ill with her nerves in Petersburg the doctor told me I must not
cross her wishes.”

“I do not think that Olva has ever frequented night clubs?”

His tones were calm, but there was a challenging ring in his voice.

The Countess shook her head. “It may not be so, but I wish that it
were so, because I have fear. You do not understand, M. Barling, the
dangers which we must run, even in your good London.” A sigh that was
nearly a sob shook her body. “Olva has no fear,” she added, “but I--I
am made of fear altogether.”

She looked, he thought, a pathetic figure in her loneliness. And
especially so because, in spite of her anxieties, she managed to
retain the dignity of her caste and its stoical acceptance of Fate.
When he spoke again his voice was very gentle:

“Will you tell me, Countess,” he asked, “just what it is that you
fear? Perhaps, if I knew, I might be better able to help you.”

She shook her head.

“I do not know what I fear. Olva has not told me--anything.”

“She is not mixed up with a political plot of any sort?”

The Countess seemed to shrink back in her chair. Instinctively she
drew the folds of the cloak she was wearing more tightly about her.
The memory of Siberia had not yet faded from her thoughts.

“It may be so,” she whispered. “I do not know.”

Bob rose and stood by the fireplace. His gray eyes were as full of
trouble as her eyes, but a resolute line had hardened at the corners
of his mouth.

“I must tell you, Countess,” he declared in stern tones, “that last
night I asked Olva to marry me, and she consented. I wanted to speak
to you about it at once, but she said she was very tired and would not
invite me to come into the house.”

As he spoke, the old lady had leaned forward in her chair. Her face
lost its weariness, and an eager expression transformed it.

“Ah,” she said, “but I am so glad--so glad. My little Olva, when she
is married to you, will be a Russian no longer.” She stopped and her
happiness faded as suddenly as it had come. Her eyes seemed to search
the room. She exclaimed, “You will find her for me, is it not so,
because you love her?”

“I swear that I shall not rest a moment until I have found her.”

He gave the Countess a description of what had happened the night
before, and of the appearance of the man with the scarred face, hoping
that she might be able to identify him. But she knew nothing of these
acquaintances of her daughter. Whatever the nature of Olva’s private
business might be, she kept it scrupulously to herself.

He waited until the old lady had become somewhat reassured, and then
drove back to Curzon Street. An hour later, and looking as fresh as
though he had spent the night in bed, he entered his car and told the
driver to take him to the High Court Hotel. It was just eight o’clock.

When he reached the hotel he went at once to the inquiry office.

“May I trouble you,” he said to the clerk on duty, “to send up my card
to one of your visitors whose name has, for the moment, escaped my
memory. He has a deep scar on his left cheek, and is a Russian.”

The man behind the desk raised his head sharply.

“M. Danatoff.”

“Ah, yes. That is his name. Perhaps you can tell him on the phone that
Mr. Barling, of fifty Curzon Street, would like to speak to him.”

The man turned to the private exchange behind him. He put a stop into
one of the holes and raised the receiver.

“Mr. Danatoff?”

He gave the message and then turned to the visitor.

“Mr. Danatoff says that he will come down in a few minutes and see you
in the lounge, sir.”

He signed to a waiting page boy to conduct Bob to that apartment. It
was quite empty. The young man sat down and lit a cigarette. At least,
he had taken a first step in getting into touch with Danatoff, who
certainly knew more about Olva’s doings than anybody else whom it
would be possible to approach.

He wondered how the man would take his visit, and whether or not he
would show embarrassment. His attitude might furnish an important clew
to the mystery which just now seemed to be so impenetrable. He got up
and strode across the room to the window. Even supposing that it was
true that Olva was mixed up with some political plot, why should this
impose on her the necessity of running away from home without even
giving her mother--to say nothing of himself--an indication of her
whereabouts.

England was a free country, in which anybody who was not a criminal
could go and come without restraint. He tried to think of some
possible set of circumstances which might afford a reason for her
strange conduct. He could think of none. Yet he was sure that she had
not acted lightly in what she had done.

Olva’s was a gentle, sympathetic nature, which made the hurting of any
living thing impossible to her. She would, he knew, have spared her
friends the terrible anxiety they were now suffering had it lain
within her power to do so.

That idea brought him no comfort, because it only served to emphasize
the urgency of the call which the girl he loved had obeyed. She had
not dared to resist, not even to delay. And she had been forced to
bury the secret, whatever it might be, in her own heart. The sight of
her pale, drawn face as the lamplight had given it to him the night
before rose in his mind. He caught his breath in the bitterness of his
spirit.

He glanced at his watch. A quarter of an hour had passed since
Danatoff had promised to come to him. He walked out to the entrance
hall and was about to inquire whether the man he sought had appeared
when a young fellow, coming from the direction of the lift, tripped
and sank on his knee beside him. He bent and assisted him to rise. As
he did so the stranger fixed on him a pair of eyes the sight of which
made him gasp. They blazed, though whether with fury or excitement he
was unable to say. Instinctively he shrank back from that terrible
gaze.

“I thank you.”

A smile curled the stranger’s lips and the fire in his eyes was
quenched. Bob advanced to the clerk and asked him to send up and say
that he was in a great hurry.

When the page had gone on this errand he strolled across to the front
entrance of the hotel. He watched with listless eyes the people
entering and leaving, men and women of many types with this in
common--that they accepted the best of life as their inalienable
right. In his own bitterness of spirit he almost hated them for their
complacency.

The page who had gone to Danatoff’s room emerged from the lift and
came to him.

“Mr. Danatoff must have gone out, sir,” he announced. “His bedroom is
empty.”




 CHAPTER V.
 THE FIRST CLEW

“He can’t have gone out.”

Bob’s voice was raised for once almost to a shout. The clerk at the
desk glanced up anxiously from his work.

“He is not in his room, sir.”

The page had the insistence of all pages. He would go on saying that
till the very end of time.

The young man strode across to the desk.

“Mr. Danatoff answered you on the phone, didn’t he?” he demanded in
anxious tones.

“Yes, sir.”

“You are quite sure that you got his message correctly--about waiting
for him in the lounge?”

The man stiffened slightly.

“I am absolutely sure, sir.”

“It’s very strange. The boy you sent up says that he has gone out. His
room is empty.”

“That is scarcely possible, sir. I have watched everyone coming from
the lifts and I could scarcely have missed him, I think. I’ll send up
again.”

He called an older man and gave him instructions to inquire from the
servants on the floor the exact movements of the Russian.

“It is possible, you see, that he may have ordered a bath before you
called.”

The man was absent only a few minutes. He confirmed the page’s
statement. Mr. Danatoff had certainly gone out. Indeed, one of the
chambermaids, who was making the bed in the next room to his, had
heard his door open some time before and seen him pass on his way to
the lift.

Bob glanced at the clerk as this piece of evidence was given. He
looked utterly bewildered.

“I can’t understand it, sir,” he declared. “But possibly he may have
passed when I was speaking to some one else. There have been one or
two inquiries here. Still, I kept my eyes open.”

“I fear there is no doubt about it.”

Bob’s voice was weary with chagrin and disappointment.

“Shall I give him a message from you when he returns?”

The young man shook his head.

“I’m afraid,” he said, “that in the circumstances he is not likely to
return--for the present, at any rate.”

He handed gratuities to the servants who had helped him, and left the
hotel. He told his driver to take him home again at once.

Now, for the first time in his life, he was absolutely at a loss what
to do next. The confidence in his own powers to trace the missing girl
which he had felt when he set out from Curzon Street had utterly
deserted him. It was obvious that Nature had not included the gifts
necessary to a successful detective among those with which she had
endowed him. He cursed himself for being a fool, a dolt.

Of course, the midnight visitor to South Street would not wish to be
interviewed by anybody, least of all by a man whose name had already
been coupled in society with that of Olva Vorloff. Any idiot might
have guessed that he would act exactly as he had acted, and so make
good his escape from the hotel. If only he had had the sense to wait
in the entrance hall. Then, at least, he might have tracked his man
down and got to grips with him.

His butler informed him that breakfast was served. He went to the
dining room. The morning papers were laid ready and he glanced through
them as he ate, a proceeding which caused his servants to exchange
glances. As a rule their master breakfasted as fastidiously as he
dined. The papers recorded the fact that a certain Grand Duke had
proclaimed himself Tsar of Russia. He pushed them away and went back
to his smoking room.

What was he to do? What did the police do in such circumstances? There
seemed to be nothing to indicate any first step. The thought crossed
his mind that it was a pity Olva had made the request that the police
should not, in any circumstances, be called in. Had she not done that
he would have gone at once to Scotland Yard and put the matter in
competent hands.

It was difficult to understand why she should fear the British police
so greatly, because she must know quite well that there was all the
difference in the world between their methods and those of the
terrible Russian _Tcheka_. Her political activities, supposing that
she was dabbling in politics, were not crimes in England. Unless,
indeed--

The fear clutched his heart so suddenly that he felt himself grow
pale. Then the blood surged again to his face. It was
impossible--impossible. Nevertheless, they had murdered her father in
cold blood--and her friends. They would have murdered her mother and
herself had not Providence intervened.

He tried to place himself in that position, the position of a man who
had seen his father led away to the slaughter, to the torture of a
mock trial, and the ignominy of secret murder. The position of a man
whose family and caste had been proscribed and trampled in the dust.

Would he cherish, in these circumstances, the ideas about murder
which, as a free Englishman, had been born in his blood? The
Bolshevist devils were established in London; they had been received
with honor. He had heard that the whole country was honeycombed with
their spies. Might it not seem even an act of piety to the daughter of
their innocent victim?

He paced the room as the terrible possibility took shape in his mind.
His gray eyes were full of anguish now--the anguish of a strong man
who finds himself helpless in the hands of Fate. If only he could
reach her! If only he could talk to her and dissuade her, or, at the
very worst, stand beside her and shield her from the dangers which
threatened!

It was horrible to think that while he remained impotent, like a
chained creature, she might be taking steps which no power on earth
could enable her to retrace. He had a vision of her gentle, tragic
beauty face to face with a new catastrophe. It wrung his heart. Was
there no quarter to which he could turn for help?

He sat down and pressed his hands on his brow, forcing himself to
think. At least he knew that she had gone westward--probably toward
Knightsbridge. That might bring her to Kensington, or take her farther
to Hammersmith and Kew. Suppose that she had gone to attend some
secret meeting, she must have returned long ago. Yet she did not
expect to return, clearly. So that this journey had been planned
beforehand, with a definite object in view. Wherever she had gone she
had remained.

He started up. If he could find the cabman who had driven her he might
achieve real progress! That, he told himself, should not be difficult,
because at that hour of the night girls do not often drive away from
houses in the West End. The man would be certain to remember his
beautiful fare.

He worked at that exhausting task for an hour and obtained no
satisfaction. Either the man who answered his call was quite sure that
none of the usual frequenters of the rank had driven Olva, or else he
professed to know nothing about the matter. From the gruffness of some
of the replies it was made to appear that cab-ranks are normally
subjected to a good deal of practical joking in the shape of spurious
inquiries.

He had almost decided that the quest was useless when suddenly Fortune
favored him. To his usual question the answer came in a matter-of-fact
voice:

“Yes, sir; I drove the lady myself. I remember it quite well. Down
Park Lane, sir, and on through Knightsbridge.”

Bob’s hands shook.

“Where did you take her to?”

“To a garage in the Hammersmith Broadway.”

“Look here, my man, if you will come now, at once, to fifty Curzon
Street--Mr. Barling’s house--I’ll give you five pounds, with possibly
more to follow.”

He hung up the receiver. His expression had changed from utter
despondency to resolute eagerness. Here at last was a definite clew,
something he could work on immediately. He would get the man to drive
him straight to Hammersmith, the garage, and begin his inquiries on
the spot.

He reached for a cigarette and lit it.

The door of the room opened and his butler entered with a card on a
silver tray. He presented the tray to his master.

Bob read:


                        DETECTIVE INSPECTOR BILES
                            New Scotland Yard


He staggered to his feet with a suppressed exclamation of horror.

“Quickly! Show him in!” he cried to the astonished servant.




 CHAPTER VI.
 WHAT BILES KNEW

Inspector Biles was a tall, lean man whose eyes and mouth seemed to
be very widely separated. This peculiarity of his face gave him a
cold, rather inhuman appearance, yet he was, in fact, a very good
fellow, if a trifle severe in his point of view. He glanced round the
exquisite room in which he found himself noting much in little time,
according to the manner of his profession. Then he looked squarely
into Bob’s eyes.

The latter, who was holding his card between finger and thumb, bowed.

“To what do I owe the honor of your visit, inspector?” His tones were
not impolite, but neither were they cordial. The detective’s eyes
flickered.

“To an event which took place this morning at the High Court Hotel,”
he said in quiet tones. As he spoke he watched the young man closely
and noted that he started.

“Well?”

“The room occupied by a certain Mr. Danatoff, a Russian, was broken
into and, or so we have reason to believe, certain papers belonging to
him stolen. It has been reported to us that while this robbery was
going on you were awaiting that gentleman in the hotel lounge.”

“Good God, so that is the explanation!”

Bob drew a deep breath. He motioned his visitor to a chair and offered
him a box of cigars. Biles sat down, but declined to smoke.

“In the circumstances I felt that it might be within your power to
give us some information. I should tell you in the first place,
however, that the robbery was not discovered until you had left the
hotel. A servant then noticed that several of the drawers in the room
had been forced open. Their contents had been rummaged, evidently by
some one in a very considerable hurry.”

“So that Danatoff was not there at all.”

“Apparently not.”

The young man lit one of his thin cigarettes. The vision of a pair of
wild eyes, looking into his own, rose suddenly and dramatically in his
memory.

“It is rather a complicated story,” he said, “but I can tell you this
much without betraying any confidence--that I had special and urgent
reasons for wishing to talk to the Russian. I got the clerk to ring up
his bedroom and give my name. He said he would see me in the lounge. I
waited there, but he did not put in an appearance. The boy who was
sent up to his room found it empty.”

He drew a deep whiff of smoke and allowed it to pass slowly between
his lips. The detective remained silent a moment. Then he declared:

“You will pardon me if I say that leaves us pretty much where we stood
before. Possibly you may not have realized in what direction my
thoughts are turned.”

He paused. Bob’s face remained completely impassive.

“The thief was in possession of the key of Mr. Danatoff’s bedroom
door. That is certain, because the door was observed to be shut a few
minutes before, and it cannot be opened except by key. It would seem
to follow that he must have obtained the key from Mr. Danatoff
himself.”

“Yes.”

“And yet, it seems unlikely that any one would voluntarily surrender
such an object to a man who, having obtained possession of it, so
greatly abused his possession.”

There was a cold ring in Biles’s voice which chilled his hearer in
spite of himself.

“You mean,” he exclaimed, leaning forward in his chair in an attitude
of tense expectancy.

“That the evidence available at present points to a theft of the key
rather than to a voluntary surrender of it.”

Bob nodded. He removed the ash from his cigarette against the side of
an exquisite little silver tray of Indian workmanship.

“But in that case,” he said, “why has not the Russian returned to
report the theft?”

“Exactly. It is the question I have already asked myself.”

The detective’s eyes seemed to be riveted on the face of his
companion. The young man felt the sense of fear which had haunted him
earlier in the morning return with new intensity. The truth, as this
policeman already saw it, began to rise starkly before him.

“You mean that you suspect foul play?”

Biles raised his left shoulder very slightly in a gesture which was
habitual with him.

“That, perhaps,” he declared, “would be putting it rather high. But
you will admit that the possibility cannot be overlooked in the
present state of our knowledge, especially as Mr. Danatoff is reputed
to be concerned in various political matters of a more or less
dangerous kind. In any case, he seems to have left the hotel twice
during the small hours of last night. On the second occasion he did
not return.”

Again Bob was aware of the close scrutiny of the blue eyes opposite to
him; but he felt also that the information this man desired of him
must not be accorded. At all costs he would protect the secret of the
woman he loved. He rose and stood in front of the fire.

“I’m afraid,” he said, “that I cannot help you there, inspector. My
business was of a purely private character. Of course, if there is
anything I can do to make your task easier, you have only to command
me.”

It was obvious that he was anxious to bring the interview to an end,
but Biles made no movement to gratify his wishes.

“I regret,” he said in slow tones, “that it should be my duty to do
so, but I have one or two questions to ask before I go which I must
urge you to answer as fully and candidly as possible.”

His words had a menacing ring. Bob felt himself flush to the eyes.

“What-- Do you?”

He restrained himself just in time.

“Believe me, I do not.” The detective’s expression remained absolutely
impassive. “I accuse nobody. I am merely asking, as I must ask, for
such information as I can obtain.”

“Very well. Go on.”

Biles closed his eyes and opened them again suddenly.

“You have known Mr. Danatoff for some time?” he asked.

“I have never spoken a word to him in my life.”

“Yet you were present, were you not, on the first occasion when he
left the High Court Hotel last night?”

The young man started violently.

“How can you know that?” he exclaimed.

“Because you stopped your cab at the hotel entrance in order to avoid
being seen by the man you were following. The porter on duty saw you;
he happens to know you by sight.”

The Scotland Yard man had not raised his voice in the slightest. He
continued in the same level tones.

“Not unnaturally, the man’s curiosity was aroused. When Mr. Danatoff
returned he went out and entered into conversation with his cabman
who, it appears, frequently ranks his cab opposite the High Court
Hotel. The cabman stated that his fare had called at number ten South
Street, which as I think you know, is the house occupied at present by
Countess Vorloff and her daughter.”

Bob had managed to regain his self-possession. He tossed the end of
his cigarette into the fireplace.

“I can only repeat,” he declared, “that I am entirely unacquainted
with Mr. Danatoff, and that my reason for taking an interest in his
movements was a personal one.”

“I should like, nevertheless, to know that reason.”

Biles’s voice was just a trifle more commanding than it had been. His
companion faced him with admirable candor.

“I’m afraid,” he said, “that I couldn’t tell you.”

The detective’s eyes narrowed menacingly.

“You see, Mr. Barling,” he snapped, “it comes to this. The whole of
London society knows that you have been constantly with Countess Olva
Vorloff during the past season. Gossip declares that you are about to
become engaged to her. Last night you are seen following, shall I say
shadowing, a Russian who calls in the small hours at her house and
then mysteriously disappears after, apparently, having surrendered the
key of his bedroom to a thief.”

He paused. Bob nodded.

“Quite so,” he said lightly. “I can see what you are driving at. I am
the jealous lover who has discovered a mysterious rival and means to
sift the matter to the bottom--even to the extent of searching my
rival’s papers for letters, and, if necessary, hitting him over the
head in order to do so.”

He broke off. Biles’s face had become very stern.

“That is not my idea,” he declared. “From the information in my
possession it is certain that you played no part in Danatoff’s
disappearance. But the same cannot be said so confidently of the
Countess Olva.”

“What are you insinuating?”

The young man had no coolness now. His face was thrust forward; his
hands twitched.

“I am insinuating nothing, but, as you know, this lady disappeared
from her home last evening before Danatoff called on her. She left a
letter for Danatoff. On receipt of that letter he also disappeared. I
have myself been to South Street and confirmed these facts.”

“Well?”

“These two are Russians. In political affairs papers, documents,
frequently possess the very highest value. It may be necessary, on
occasion, to go to any length, all lengths, to obtain--shall we say a
plan, or a list of names.”

“I see.”

The young man’s face had become as grim as that of his interlocutor.
Once again he had himself under control.

“In other words Mr. Barling, what were the instructions about
following Danatoff and about visiting the High Court Hotel this
morning which you received from the Countess Olva?”

Bob drew a deep breath. “That,” he declared, “is a question I can
answer. I received absolutely no instructions of any sort from her
about this man or any other man. What is more, she has never so much
as mentioned his name in my hearing.”

Biles rose. “Thank you,” he said crisply. He turned to the door.




 CHAPTER VII.
 OLVA’S MIDNIGHT DRIVES

As soon as the detective had left him Bob sank down into one of his
armchairs. He had managed to retain his courage in front of the
fellow, but now, when the ordeal had ended, his courage seemed to ebb.
The stark facts of the situation assailed him with overwhelming force.

And there was added to them another, new consideration: If he
attempted to find the woman he loved he might be the means, however
unwitting, of betraying her into the hands of her enemies. He rose at
that thought and rang the bell. For the future he would act with new
circumspection, with all the care and caution of which he was capable.
The determination seemed to bring back his habitual coolness of
manner.

A footman answered his summons.

“Has the cab I ordered come for me?” he asked.

“Yes, sir. It is waiting now at the door, sir.”

It was evident that the man was greatly surprised that his master
should have summoned a cab in this way. But his impassive features
remained impassive. Bob’s servants were reputed the most excellent in
London.

“Good. See that he doesn’t go away. Tell him I shall come in a few
minutes.”

The servant retired, more astonished than ever. Bob mounted the stairs
to his bedroom and changed into an old lounge suit of dark blue. He
told his butler that he didn’t know when he would be back.

The cabman, when he interrogated him on the pavement, was quite
certain about his fare of the night before. He had taken a very
considerable interest in the lady when she told him to drive to a
garage at that strange hour of the night. He described her so
accurately that Bob had not the slightest doubt of his _bona fides_.
The young man gave him the five pounds which he had promised and told
him to drive him at once to Hammersmith.

The vehicle was an old one and ground and grated in its gears as it
lurched along, but he was too preoccupied to notice that. Like most
fastidious people, he could easily forget his fastidiousness when the
occasion demanded attention to more serious matters. He wondered what
he should do when he reached the garage. And then the thought came to
him that, after all, Inspector Biles had been able to obtain an
immense amount of information of real value in a very short space of
time.

He was an expert, no doubt, but it was an article of their creed,
strengthened overwhelmingly during the years of his war service, that
what one man can do another can do also, given the will and the
courage. He had both, in this case, for there could not be the
slightest doubt that the girl he loved had the most urgent need for
his help.

The garage was quite a small one. It was situated in a street opening
on the Hammersmith Broadway. He told his cabman to wait for him and
walked into the yard. A young fellow about his own age came to meet
him.

“I am right in thinking,” he began, drawing a bow at a venture, “that
the Countess Olva Vorloff keeps her car here?”

The man stared at him and then shook his head.

“No, sir. One or two ladies keep their cars here, but no one of that
name.”

Bob bit his lip. It was certain that Olva had come to the garage. The
cabman was quite positive, too, that he had seen her enter it.

“The lady I mean,” he said, “is fair with rather a pale face. She came
here last night very late.”

The man’s expression changed.

“Oh, that’s Miss Seymour,” he declared. “She has kept her car here for
a year or more. Goes out often at night, she does.”

There was a hint of mystery in the tones, which the young man
resented. So Olva had an assumed name for her midnight escapades! He
drew a sharp breath.

“She went out as usual last night, I take it?” he asked.

“Yes, sir. About two o’clock or thereabouts. Mostly she’s a bit
earlier than that. Seemed upset about something or other, she did. But
it ain’t our business, of course, to ask questions of our customers.”
The man hesitated a moment, then he inquired, “You’ll be from the
police, perhaps?”

“No.”

He seemed disappointed at this information. It was evident that he
fully expected official inquiries, sooner or later, into the strange
habits of his client. Again Bob felt his resentment stirring.

“Miss Seymour did not tell you where she was going?” he demanded in
cold accents.

“Oh, no, sir. She never does tell that to anybody. Just walks in here
and asks if the car--it’s a little Hillman coupé she has--is ready;
gets in--starts the engine, and away.”

“I see. And how long is she usually about on these occasions?”

The man hesitated. Bob realized that he was watching him narrowly and
guessed that the impression that he was a detective from Scotland Yard
remained in spite of his denial.

“The time varies a bit. Sometimes it’s as short as four hours,
sometimes it’s eight hours or more.” He broke off and lowered his
voice. “But the distance don’t vary any.”

“How do you mean?”

“Well, sir, it’s a bit queer, as you will allow, when a young lady
like ’er goes off drivin’ alone in the middle of the night. Makes yer
think, it does. So I took the notion, a few weeks back, of seeing just
how many miles she ’appens to cover in ’er journeys. There’s a
mileometer on the car.”

“Yes?” The eagerness in Bob’s voice was but feebly repressed.

“Well, the first time I watched it was sixty-eight miles and four
furlongs; the second time it was sixty-eight miles and three furlongs,
and the third time it was sixty-eight miles and two furlongs. That
should mean that ’er destination is thirty-four miles from ’ere or
near about.”

Bob took a tiny engagement book from his pocket and made a note of the
figures.

“Did you notice anything else?” he asked.

“Yes, sir. Each time the car ’as come back it ’as ’ad red dust on the
inside of the wings--like the stuff they make carriage drives of.”

“So.” Bob glanced round the yard while he racked his brain to think of
any other question which it would be useful to ask. His eye
encountered a big road map of the South of England hanging on the
wall, an advertisement of some petrol firm. “By the way, do you know
the country well round about London?”

“No, sir, I don’t.”

“Let me see. Thirty-four miles. How far out would that takes us? And,
by the way, which direction did Miss Seymour take?”

“Always the same--west along the Broadway.”

He noted that fact also. Then he returned his book to his pocket.

“Thank you,” he said. “You have saved me a great deal of trouble, I
think. Is this your own garage, or are you only employed here?”

“It’s my father’s garage.”

Bob slipped a Treasury note into the young fellow’s hand and returned
to his cab. He told the driver to take him back to Curzon Street. On
the way he tried to think of the roads leading out of London to the
west. There was the Great West Road to Staines, and the Bath Road, and
north of that again, the main road to Oxford.

Going thirty miles out on these three main arteries one would come to,
respectively, the neighborhoods of Ascot, Maidenhead, and High
Wycombe. But there were so many crossroads and inter-crossroads that
that information seemed to be nearly valueless. He wondered if many of
the houses round London had red carriage drives.

It was just possible that Cane, his chauffeur, might know. He had been
employed for a time at Amersham, in Bucks, and was a most observant
fellow. Bob determined to have him in and ask him the moment he got
home.

Then his mind changed to the strange journeys which Olva was
apparently in the habit of making. He could understand her taking an
interest in the politics of the Russian colony in London. But why
should that interest compel her to make secret excursions to the
country? What was it which occurred at the house with the red carriage
drive? He sat up on the springless cushions and gazed with distracted
eyes at the people in the streets.

When he reached home he went at once to the telephone and rang up a
number on the Mayfair exchange.

“Is that Dr. Hailey’s house? This is Mr. Robert Barling speaking. I
wonder if I could see the doctor this afternoon? Yes, my business is
most urgent. Very urgent indeed.”




 CHAPTER VIII.
 THE AFTERNOON CALLER

When he left Bob Barling, Inspector Biles took a cab to South
Street, to the Vorloff’s house. He had already visited that house but
there were one or two supplementary points on which he desired further
information. It was his most cherished professional principle that no
detail is too small to be worth verifying.

He was shown into the Countess’s boudoir by Peter Nikolaiovitch, who
appeared to regard his return as ill-omened. The man belonged to the
tall, lean type of Russian. He had the remote melancholy of his race,
the dogged, faithful melancholy which sent battalions of the Russian
army during the Great War unarmed against the rifles and guns of the
Germans. Biles glanced at his pale cheeks and noted that he was
disturbed; he made up his mind to question him. On his first visit he
had spoken only to the Countess herself.

In the first place, however, he found himself under the necessity of
soothing the fears of that poor lady. Countess Vorloff was in great
distress and the return of the policeman quickened all her anxieties.
Her scared face was weary with the old and the new sorrows which had
come upon her. Her eyes were reddened with weeping.

“I am deeply sorry to trouble you again,” Biles said gently. “But if
we are to find your daughter we must make the fullest possible
inquiries. I am sure that you will realize this.”

“Ah yes, I will not hinder you, sir. But it is all so dark, is it not?
And already I have suffered so much.”

She bowed her head and the detective pitied her. He thought that she
was like a stately old ship driven from port and broken by the waves
and the winds. In her helplessness and weakness there was something
indescribably pathetic.

“I would like, if I may,” he suggested, “to ask a few questions of
your servants. They are Russians, are they not?”

The old woman nodded.

“Peter Nikolaiovitch, who admitted you, is a Russian,” she declared,
“though he has lived for some years in England and speaks your
language perfectly; and my maid also, Sophie Ivanovna. She has
preferred service in England to the life she might have enjoyed in
Moscow. Alas, even our service is but beggarly now! Sophie is maid to
Olva as also to myself, and Peter must drive our car as well as act
the part of our butler.”

She spoke with a resignation which was new in Biles’s experience and
which astonished him by its naïveté.

He bowed.

“May I call the man first?” he asked adding, “perhaps you will not
mind if I talk to him in another room, alone.”

At the Countess’s request he rang the bell. The servant who had
admitted him answered it. The old lady spoke to him in Russian and he
bowed low in response. He turned to Biles.

“Will you follow me, sir, if you please.”

They went to the dining room. The detective sat down and took his
notebook from his pocket. He glanced at an entry he had made and then
replaced the book. When he looked up he saw the deep eyes of the man
watching him. The conviction that the information he sought would not
easily be come by hardened in his mind.

“It was to you, was it not,” he began, “that the Countess Olva
intrusted the letter she left for M. Danatoff?”

“Yes, sir.”

“M. Danatoff is frequently at the house?”

“No, sir. He comes very seldom.”

The man seemed to hesitate a moment. Then he added: “He came last
night, in the first place, at eight o’clock, after her ladyship had
gone out. I did not see him, but the maid who answered the door gave
him the address to which she had gone, because he said his business
with her was very urgent.”

“Ah!” Biles’s eyes narrowed with eagerness.

“What address did she give him?” he asked sharply.

“Number forty-eight Brook Street. Her ladyship had gone to a dance.”

The detective nodded. That statement, as he knew was correct. So
Danatoff had been following the beautiful Russian girl all the
evening.

“Did he go to Brook Street?”

The man shook his head. “I do not know, sir.”

“He left no message on his first visit?”

“No, sir.”

“Did he telephone during the evening?”

“Some one telephoned, sir, after their ladyships returned from the
dance, but I do not know who this was, because the Countess Olva
answered the call herself.”

“You heard, perhaps, what she said?”

The man shook his head again.

“No, sir.”

Biles thought for a moment.

“How was M. Danatoff dressed when he called in the early hours of the
morning?” he asked.

“He was dressed in a brown suit and he wore a large cloak and a slouch
hat.”

“Hm! He must have changed his clothes, then, after he left the dance.”

“Yes, sir--undoubtedly.”

“So that, apparently he expected to go out after making his call?”

“Apparently, sir.”

“You saw the mark on his cheek, I take it?”

“Yes, sir.”

So far the man had retained his Slavonic calm, but it was obvious that
he was not at his ease. Biles watched him for a moment with the cold
gaze which he had often found so useful in dealing with prevaricators.
The man did not flinch.

“Tell me,” he asked, “did the Countess Olva receive any other visitor
during yesterday?”

He had no sooner spoken than a thrill of satisfaction ran along his
nerves. This time he had certainly come to the matter which the
servant was anxious to hide from him. The pale cheeks reddened and the
melancholy eyes grew resentful. There was no reply.

The detective repeated his question in somewhat severe tones, but
still Peter Nikolaiovitch maintained his silence. A frown gathered on
Biles’s brow.

“Listen to me,” he declared. “In England, as you should know, the
police do not act as they act in your native country. I have not come
here to get you to betray your mistress or her secrets. I have come to
ask you to help me to find her. The Countess Olva is perhaps in great
danger of her life; it may be that she has already lost her life.”

The man drew a deep breath.

“My mistress,” he said, “asked that I should not tell any one about
what took place yesterday afternoon.”

“At that time she could not know what was coming to her during the
night.”

Biles spoke cautiously, because he guessed that this man would not be
intimidated by any show of anger, or by any threats. He saw that his
argument had exerted some effect.

“Yet, she was very particular on that point, sir, for she asked me
again and again; oh, yes, many times.”

The detective thought a moment.

“I repeat,” he said in quiet tones, “that I am asking you this
question to help your mistress. Unless we know who her visitors have
been we cannot hope to rescue her from the danger in which she is now
almost certainly placed.”

Peter Nikolaiovitch shook his head. But this gesture was merely an
indication that he was communing with his own spirit, weighing up the
pros and cons of the course open to him. Biles recognized the fact and
awaited his decision without impatience.

“Very well, sir,” he declared at last, “I will tell you, because I
fear, as you fear, that disaster has come to her ladyship. Yesterday
afternoon about five o’clock, I admitted a visitor to her presence--a
young man.”

The detective betrayed no feeling of any sort.

“A Russian?” he asked.

“Yes, sir; I am sure of that, because I heard the first words which he
spoke when he entered the room.”

“Did he tell you his name?”

“Mr. Tsarov.”

“Tsarov? It is not a common name, is it?”

“No, sir.” The man shifted his position uneasily, as though fresh
doubts were gathering in his mind. “I think,” he added, “that her
ladyship did not know her visitor by this name, because when she saw
him she cried out in great surprise, yet I had already announced him
to her.”

Biles’s eyes narrowed.

“Do you remember the words which she made use of?”

“She cried: ‘What! you!’ The visitor answered, ‘My dear Olva, surely
you are not surprised that I also have lived through the storm?’ I did
not hear any more of their conversation, but my mistress had become
very pale and I could see that she was afraid--oh, yes, very much
afraid.”

The detective nodded.

“You did not return to the room while the stranger was there?”

“Yes, sir. Her ladyship rang the bell and told me to bring tea. As I
was entering the room I heard her say, ‘In this country crime is still
crime, remember.’ Mr. Tsarov did not reply. But when I was carrying in
the tea things a little later he exclaimed, ‘I cannot afford to be
squeamish when so much is at stake!’ I heard no more at all of their
conversation.”

Biles rose from his chair.

“There is just one other thing I wish to ask you,” he said. “Will you
describe the appearance of this Mr. Tsarov--as accurately as you can?”

“He seemed to be a young man and yet his face was not that of a boy.”
The servant hesitated a moment. “It was his eyes which I noticed most.
They were very strange, glaring eyes, which seemed to threaten those
on whom they were turned. Otherwise he was not distinguished; of
medium height, with brown hair and clean-shaven.”


The maid, Sophie Ivanovna, was a strikingly pretty girl with a mass of
short curly hair. She had big luminous eyes, set in her still face of
the type so characteristic of her people. She seemed restless under
the detective’s scrutiny, but answered his questions in a clear, firm
voice. She corroborated the man’s story in every respect.

“How many servants are there in this house?” Biles asked.

“But Peter Nikolaiovitch and myself, and a woman who comes sometimes
to clean. It is a small house. I am cook for their ladyships as well
as maid.”

When he was alone Biles made a careful note of his examination. It was
a precaution he never, by any chance, neglected. He read his notes
over after he had completed them, then he turned back to the notes he
had made earlier in the day. Everything, so far as he could see,
tended to support the idea he had formed, that a close connection
existed between the disappearance of Olva Vorloff and the burglary at
the High Court Hotel. The connecting link between these two events was
Bob Barling. It was urgently necessary that he should obtain more
information about that interesting young man.

He put his notebook back into his pocket and walked out into the hall.
Peter Nikolaiovitch was waiting for him and handed him his hat and
gloves. He signaled to a passing cab and told the driver to take him
to twenty-two Harley Street.




 CHAPTER IX.
 BARLING YIELDS HIS SECRECY

The address which he had given was that of Dr. Eustace Hailey. Biles
and Dr. Hailey were old friends. They had met, in the first instance
when the detective suffered from a nervous breakdown. Doctor Hailey’s
passion for amateur detective work had served thereafter as the link
binding them together. Moreover, his extensive knowledge of London
society had often served his friend from Scotland Yard in good stead.
It was of this last knowledge that Biles now sought to avail himself.

Dr. Hailey was busy with a patient when he reached the house, and so
he had leisure to collect his thoughts. The matter in which he
specially desired to be informed was the character of young Barling
and the names of young Barling’s friends.

He had been quite favorably impressed by the fellow himself, but his
experience was against placing too much trust on such impressions. No
one, he knew, can be more charming than your born rogue who is not
troubled by any scruples of conscience, and so is entirely at his
ease, even when his crime is but a few hours old.

Bob Barling, moreover, was the sort of man about whom women concern
themselves. The detective set a good deal of store by this. Men of
that type are always getting into scrapes which, as a rule, are none
of their making. They have some quality of mind which betrays their
judgment and sets up, instead of it, a code of honor invented by women
for their own purposes.

Consequently, they are habitually doing foolish things and putting
themselves in ridiculous positions. A woman has only to weep in order
to galvanize them into frantic states of energy or aggressiveness.

A cold smile bent his lips as this thought crossed his mind. What
fools men were! Even those in whom women took no interest, for they
always secretly envied the other kind their misfortunes. He sighed and
glanced out of the long Georgian windows at the dreary street--the
“Valley of the Shadow of Death,” as Harley Street has been ominously
nicknamed.

“Dr. Hailey will see you, sir.”

It was the smooth voice of Jenkins, the doctor’s butler, who was
reputed to be able, with a word, to mollify the most violent of his
master’s mental patients. Biles followed him to the consulting room.

His entries to that room had been legion, yet he never entered it
without experiencing a fresh thrill. Dr. Hailey always seemed so much
bigger, so much more “impressive by reason of his size”--as one of his
colleagues had put it, than he had expected. The present occasion was
no exception to this rule. The great round face, the huge shoulders,
the towering height of the man overwhelmed him anew. No wonder he was
called the “Harley Street Giant.”

And yet Dr. Hailey did not give the impression of sheer bulk which
quite small stout men often give. His bigness was proportioned like
the bigness of a ship. It was manifestly healthy, strong, robust.
Moreover, it was greatly genial. There was a kindness in the large
face, with its rather listless eyes, which was magical.

“Well, my dear Biles,” the doctor cried, “to what fresh horror do I
owe the delight of seeing you again?”

Biles took the hand extended to him and met the genial smile with the
rather acidulated expression of satisfaction which was his own nearest
approach of it.

“I’m afraid,” he declared, “that, as yet, the horror is to seek.”

He sat down in the chair which Dr. Hailey indicated and accepted a
cigar, the end of which he bit off.

“What I want to know,” he said abruptly, “is anything you can tell me
about a young man named Robert Barling--the Hon. Robert Barling.”

“What!” The doctor’s large eyes opened wide. “Surely you can’t mean
that you suspect Bob Barling of anything?”

“I didn’t say I suspected him. And I don’t--except of being a fool
where women are concerned.”

Dr. Hailey shook his head.

“There, my friend, you are greatly mistaken. Bob Barling is more
indifferent to women than any man I have ever known--except, perhaps,
yourself. I happen to know, because I attended his elder brother, the
present Lord Greenshire, for shellshock after the war, and saw a great
deal of Bob then, and he has remained one of my friends.”

The detective lit his cigar with slow deliberation.

“Is it possible,” he asked, “that you have not heard of his
infatuation for that Russian girl?”

“Olva Vorloff? Of course. But that is a serious love affair, my dear
Biles. I was speaking of women in general.”

“Serious or not, it has landed your young friend in a very awkward
predicament.”
