exactly as it was when they were handed over. My man, of course, had
opened the linings when we examined them at the station.”

The doctor took a pinch of snuff.

“That seems rather strange, doesn’t it?” he remarked in slow tones.

There was no answer. Biles asked:

“Will the coroner accept my suggestion and adjourn the inquest?”

“I think so, sir. I told him, as you directed me, that you were
anxious to avoid summoning many witnesses as yet, and he left
everything in my hands. There will only be yourself and the doctors in
addition to my own men.”




 CHAPTER XXX.
 BAITING A HOOK

The Coroner’s Court was crowded long before the hour fixed for the
inquest of Danatoff. The case was arousing an immense amount of public
interest, and all the London newspapers were represented by their
“crime experts”--eager-looking young men with reputations to make or
sustain.

Biles’s evidence created something of a sensation. His cold manner and
precise way of speaking always impressed jurors profoundly. But, in
the present instance, these were perhaps less convincing than the
array of facts and deductions which he was in a position to present.
In answer to the coroner, a local doctor, who treated him with marked
deference, he admitted frankly that there were still points in the
case which were not fully cleared up.

“The police have made no arrest so far?”

“No, sir, but from information which has reached them that may be
remedied soon, I think.”

“The murdered man had not been robbed, I take it?”

“I am not sure. I found his purse in his pocket. It contained only a
small sum of money, so that it is possible he may have carried a
notecase as well.”

Medical evidence was given as to the findings at the autopsy, and
Doctor Hailey entered the box to corroborate the statements of his
professional colleague. He made no mention of his adventures of the
previous night.

The police sergeant was the next witness. His evidence was purely
formal and related to the communications, which he had had, since the
discovery of the crime, with the estate agent of Lord Borrodean, the
owner of Moorfields. The place had not been let at all and
consequently its use was entirely unauthorized.

In answer to the coroner, the sergeant stated that the agent had come
over from Maidstone Abbey the day before and had informed him that the
padlock on the door of the garage was not that which had originally
been placed there.

At the conclusion of this statement the coroner addressed the jury. He
told them that, in his view, the facts warranted only one verdict.

“Nevertheless,” he added; “I propose, in the meantime, to adjourn the
inquest, because I am informed by the police that the ends of justice
would not be served by the hearing, at the present juncture, of
further evidence. A number of clews are now being followed up and
discussion of them could only serve to enlighten the criminals and
make the task of the police more difficult.”

When the court closed Biles and Doctor Hailey returned to the Red
Lion.

“Thank Heaven,” the detective said, “that we have got over that. I was
afraid that he might insist on finishing the case within a day or two
and dragging people down from London to give evidence. In my
experience that sort of thing, at this stage of the investigation,
always plays the mischief with our work.”

He threw himself down on the old-fashioned horse-hair sofa, which was
the principal piece of furniture in the room. He looked very tired and
rather dispirited, the doctor thought, as though the fresh
complication of the last few hours had bewildered without stimulating
him.

“So far as I can see,” he declared, “we shall get no further until we
have discovered what part this fellow Browning has played, and that
will be a job to tax all our resources. I mean to go up to London this
afternoon and organize a new search for a motor car with slight damage
to its front bumper and radiator.

“It is just possible that they may have omitted or been unable to
remove all traces of the collision. And I am going to make a close
canvass of every likely house agent. The birds may have flown, but
they have come to roost somewhere.”

His voice was not so hopeful as the words it uttered. He filled his
pipe and lit it with great deliberation.

“Remember,” Doctor Hailey said, “that the cipher is still in our
possession. Sooner or later, unless I am mistaken, there will be a
fresh effort to obtain it. That bait should be capable of concealing a
hook, surely.”

The detective contracted his brow.

“How can you set such a trap without arousing suspicion?” he demanded.

“There is the press. Every London paper is represented down here by a
so-called ‘crime expert.’ Suppose you were to announce that a cipher
had been discovered and was being examined by somebody or other famous
in this branch of investigation?”

“Um!” Biles raised himself on his elbow. “It’s an idea, certainly. I
believe one of our own fellows did a lot of that work for the
Admiralty during the war. He’s retired now and lives at Hendon.”

Doctor Hailey was standing in front of the fire. He had his snuffbox
in his hand and helped himself to a pinch before he replied.

“Probably he would be ready to take the risk for a consideration,” he
suggested.

Half and hour later Mr. Harold Webb, of the _Daily Tribune_, was shown
into the room. Biles received him with great geniality.

“The fact is,” he confessed, “this case is giving us a good deal of
trouble. That must be my excuse for asking you to come here.”

Mr. Webb, who was a young man with a keen face and black hair oiled
back on his head, nodded sagaciously.

“I am entirely at your service,” he declared in crisp tones.




 CHAPTER XXXI.
 THE TRAIL OF A BULLET

Bob Barling had not returned to Curzon Street when Biles left the
Red Lion shortly after midday, nor had any news of him been obtained
by two o’clock in the afternoon, at which time Doctor Hailey rang up
his house.

The doctor mounted the stairs from the manager’s office with slow
steps. A sense of impending disaster weighed on his spirit. Four days
had now elapsed since the murder of Danatoff and, in spite of all that
had occurred, they seemed to be as far as ever from any solution of
the mystery.

He came to the sitting room and stood looking out of the window with
dull eyes. His car was standing in the yard below, and he wondered
whether he should or should not postpone his return to London. It was
difficult to see what end could be served by further investigation in
that bewildered field, whereas, as he knew, the scene of a murder is
always the best place to look for fresh evidence bearing on its
committal.

If the trap which Biles had set tempted Tsarov and his accomplices to
a new effort, good and well; but if it failed to tempt them the
chances of achieving any progress were very slender.

He turned away and sat down by the fire. If the Russians had not
released Bob, then the darkest anxieties as to his safety were
warranted. They would not, assuredly, for long burden themselves with
his custody at a time when the entire police force of the country were
mobilized to track them down. Tsarov had achieved his purpose and it
was certain that he had associates ready, at his bidding, to commit
any kind of devilry.

He got up and went down to the yard, where his driver was awaiting his
further orders. There were a number of people standing about, and his
big car seemed to be the attracting a good deal of attention. His eyes
gladdened at the sight of its shining panels and appearance of easy
strength.

“I think,” he told the man, “that I shall not return to London today,
after all. I wish you to take me a mile or two on the Oxford Road.”

“Very good, sir,” the man hesitated a moment. “I think it may be
difficult to get a garage for the car tonight,” he declared, “because
already they are nearly full up and there is a big local market
tomorrow.”

Doctor Hailey shrugged his shoulders. Such details at the moment, had
lost their interest for him. He entered the vehicle and lay back on
the deep cushions.

When they reached the end of the byway leading to Moorfields he told
his man to wait for him and walked toward the spot where the Hillman
coupé had been left standing. The police had driven it away, but the
wheel-tracks still showed clearly on the soft surface of the road.

He examined them again without reaching any new conclusion. Then he
struck up toward the house along the line taken by Olva Vorloff. He
examined the ground and the surrounding bushes and trees very closely
as he went. On several occasions he turned and looked back toward the
roadway.

It was clear that if Olva had been escaping from some pursuer she must
have been well within his sight during the whole of her passage from
car to house. The bushes were small and the trees widely separated and
becoming bare of foliage. He bent and examined one of the footprints,
now grown a trifle faint.

There could be no doubt that his early impression was fully justified.
She had been running as fast as her legs would carry her. He
straightened himself and took a pinch of snuff. It was his experience
that a frightened woman always quickens her pace, just as a frightened
man almost invariably tries to control his feelings by walking slower.

Yet what had the girl feared? It could scarcely be Danatoff, since he
had come to Moorfields, surely, at her own invitation. That disposed
of the possible bearing on the case of the second line of footsteps
further down the wood, which were his.

Nor could it well be Tsarov, since there was absolutely nothing to
show that he had entered the wood at all. And, in any case, her
attitude to that man in the house in Brunswick Road made it
sufficiently obvious that her feelings toward him were not actuated by
deadly fear.

There remained only Browning, that mysterious individual who seemed to
have played so many parts in this drama without affording the smallest
clew to his identity. Browning had certainly come down from the house
on foot, since he had actually been seen by the marketmen walking in
the high road. Was it conceivable that he had encountered the girl
leaving her car and threatened her?

He sighed deeply and turned round again to see if the place where he
was standing was visible from the whole length of the byroad.

And then suddenly he started forward.

He came with swift strides to one of the trees which flanked his path
and set his gaze, now grown eager and alert, on a white scar situated
about six feet up the brown trunk. He put his eyeglass in his eye and
contemplated the mark with deliberate care.

There could be no doubt at all. The scar had been made by a bullet.
Its surface, where the bark had been stripped off, was traversed by a
narrow line, a kind of groove, which could have been produced in no
other fashion.

A further inspection of the wood convinced him that the bullet had
been fired from below and not from above. That is to say, it had been
fired by some one standing at or near the place where Danatoff’s
foot-prints had been found.

Once again eagerness gave place to bewilderment and perplexity. Here
was evidence which, in other circumstances, he would have described as
convincing, indeed as conclusive, to show that Danatoff had certainly
fired at the girl as she ran from him. Yet by no stretch of the
imagination could he bring himself to accept it unless he changed his
whole conception of the crime and looked at Olva, not as the
aggressor, but as the person threatened.




 CHAPTER XXXII.
 LUCK IS A TRUMP

Was it possible to do that? Was it conceivable that the girl had
fled from home in order to escape the attentions or threats of this
man and that he had divined her purpose and followed her? But, if so,
why had she strolled with him in Bond Street the same morning, and why
had he passed her without recognition at the Brook Street dance? The
letter which she had left for her mother was certainly not the kind of
missive which a woman in deadly fear was likely to write.

He dismissed that theory and returned to the place where he had first
noticed the scar on the tree. He examined the ground here with special
care and soon had his reward.

Faintly outlined on the earth, close to one of the footmarks, were the
imprints of a hand with outstretched fingers. So the girl had stumbled
when the shot was fired at her.

He drew a sharp breath. That, of course, was the explanation of the
fact that her head, when he saw her in Hampstead, was swathed in
bandages!

He looked toward the house, wondering whether the assailant might have
been standing in the main avenue, but a glance was sufficient to
dispose of that idea. The bullet had come from below--on that point,
at least, there was no room for doubt of any sort. Moreover, it had
wounded the girl. In other words, Olva, when she reached the house,
must have been in a very sore plight.

In that plight, wounded and haunted by deadly fear, she had plunged
her knife in Danatoff’s breast.

He recalled his own feelings when regaining consciousness after the
blow which Tsarov had struck him. At such a moment one did not
distinguish very clearly between friend and foe. If Danatoff had been
awaiting her in the yard--

But would she, in that case, have been armed with the terrible knife
which he had himself extracted from the dead man’s chest?

He walked on slowly to the yard, which seemed now strangely empty and
deserted. He could arrive at no conclusion of any sort. Olva’s wound,
whatever its immediate effect might have been, had been slight enough
apparently.

It was just possible, too, that Danatoff had been attacked at the time
when his car collided with the Hillman coupé and had managed, in the
first instance, to beat off his assailant. In that case he would
certainly fire at any one within sight--especially in the
semi-darkness of a night illuminated only by a half moon.

He identified over again the various spots in the yard which had borne
witness to the events of the tragedy. Then he took from his pocket the
sketches he had made on his last visit and attempted to correct them
in the light of more recent information.

That recalled to his mind the fact that Danatoff was certainly armed,
as the bullet marks on the wall had demonstrated. The murdered man,
perhaps, in pursuing Olva, ran straight into a trap. But no! Because
his footsteps showed that he had turned back at the edge of the wood
and gone down again to his car.

Doctor Hailey had further recourse to his snuffbox. No wonder this
case had forced Biles to confess that even his cool head was turned by
it. The puzzle seemed to defy every attempt at solution.

And yet, as he reflected, there must be some explanation into which
all these diverse scraps of evidence would fit accurately. Detective
work was exactly like scientific research work. There was always a
master-key to be discovered which, when found, would unlock all the
different problems.

He thought of Sir Ronald Ross, toiling in the blazing heat of an
Indian jungle to find the mosquito which is the bearer from victim to
victim of the terrible malaria poison; and of how, at last, when
strength and even hope were at a point of exhaustion, the very insect
he sought among all the thousands of its related types, settled in
front of his eyes on the wall of the room in which he was working.

He glanced up with new resolution on his face and once more let his
eyes roam across the tiles at his feet.

A moment later he stooped down and picked up the end of a black shoe
lace to which the metal point still adhered.

He stood contemplating his find during many minutes. The lace seemed
to be comparatively old, and he guessed that no considerable amount of
force had been exerted in breaking it.

Then a look of fresh eagerness came into his eyes.

He returned quickly to his car and told his driver to take him to the
police station in Wycombe.




 CHAPTER XXXIII.
 ONE MAN IS GUILTY

As soon as he reached the police station, Doctor Hailey asked for
the sergeant and was conducted to a small office behind the outer one,
where that good man sat, pen in hand, in his shirt sleeves, writing a
report.

He sprang to his feet as the doctor entered.

“You have the shoe which Danatoff was wearing when he was murdered?”
Doctor Hailey asked in the crisp tones he always assumed when his mind
was working quickly.

“Yes, doctor. It was found with the rest of the clothes by the
roadside.”

“The body has not yet been buried?”

“No, sir; not till tomorrow.” The man sighed. “I’m afraid,” he added,
“that it will have to be a parish funeral. We have found no relatives
or friends.”

He spoke in low tones as though, in his mind, this form of disposal of
a human body represented the very last degree of humiliation.

“Can I see the body?”

The policeman hesitated at this request, which his instinct told him
was irregular. But Doctor Hailey was Biles’s colleague in this affair
and, above all things, especially in view of the disaster of the
bundle of clothing, he was anxious to stand well with the Scotland
Yard man.

“It has been handed over to the parochial authorities for burial,” he
declared, with just a trace of formality in his tones.

Doctor Hailey frowned slightly.

“Is it still lying in the mortuary?”

“Yes, sir.”

“I should like to see it.”

The sergeant’s hesitation gave place to compliance. He conducted the
doctor to the house of the mortuary-keeper and summoned that official
to lend his assistance.

They came to the low building which Doctor Hailey had already visited
at the time of the autopsy.

As the door opened a breath of cold, damp air assailed their nostrils,
air unsunned and stagnant, which seemed to be charged with the
exhalations of death. The sergeant raised his voice instinctively, as
though he desired to cheer himself, but the silence of the long room,
with its pale tables, absorbed all the tones.

Danatoff’s body had been placed in a plain black coffin--the kind of
coffin, Doctor Hailey thought, which is white on the underside, where
a thrifty authority will have stayed its hand in the expenditure of
paint. The paint seemed to have sunk into the wood, giving it an
appearance similar to that resulting when an ink-bottle is spilled on
a deal table.

The coffin lid had been screwed down, but the screws were not many and
were quickly removed. Doctor Hailey turned to the policeman and took
the shoe which he was carrying. He raised one of the dead feet and put
the shoe on it.

The shoe fitted pretty well, but it was slightly too wide, so that its
sides lay together over the top of the foot even before the laces had
been drawn taut. Such a shoe must, sooner or later, blister such a
foot at the heel, and yet Danatoff’s foot showed no sign of having
been blistered.

He handed gratuities to the two men and announced that he was
satisfied with his investigation. He walked back to the hotel, taking
the shoe with him. The other shoe, which Biles had employed to test
the foot-marks in the wood, was still in the private sitting room. He
examined it and saw that its lace, like that of its neighbor, was
intact.

So the broken lace had not come from this pair, at any rate.

He no longer doubted its origin in the smallest degree. It had come
from the shoes which Danatoff was wearing when he reached Moorfields,
but which had been removed from his feet after the murder was
committed.

The owner of the shoes he was now looking at had, clearly, possessed
bigger feet than his victim, broader feet. It had been necessary for
him when he effected the exchange of footwear with Danatoff to strain
the laces of the latter’s shoes. In doing that he had broken one of
them.

He sat down beside the fire and closed his eyes. So, after all, it was
not Danatoff who had pursued Olva up through the wood to the house and
fired at her as she ran. And it was not Tsarov either, because he had
distinctly noticed the small hands and feet of that man.

There was only Browning left, and he tried to visualize the part
played by this actor when, suddenly, his thoughts assumed fantastic
shapes and began to make riot with his reason. An immense weariness,
the aftermath of the adventures at Hampstead and of his labors during
the day, stole over his brain.

When he awoke the fire had gone out and the room was cold. He stumbled
to his feet and switched on the light. It was nearly eight o’clock. He
rang the bell and told the servant who answered it to relight the fire
and order his dinner.

“And I will have the wine list, please.”

Another servant brought the list to him. He scanned it carefully and
then raised his eyebrows in sudden satisfaction. He had not expected
to find _Chateau Yquem_ included in this cellar.

“I will have half a bottle of that vintage,” he declared, indicating
the choice wine with his finger.

He ate his dinner slowly, struggling all the while to resist the
seductions of an almost overwhelming desire to go to sleep again. The
splendid claret glowed under the lamp in his glass, casting a golden
shadow on the white tablecloth. Somehow that appearance seemed to be
charged with good omen, as though a sign was given him of coming
enlightenment.

He raised the wine to his lips and sipped it thriftily, experiencing
its exquisite bouquet and delicately aromatic flavor with the joy of a
lover of wine whose palate has never been spoiled by tobacco smoke.
Its genial spirit wooed him from his depression and rekindled his
wits. He told himself that he had narrowed the issue to a single
necessity--the finding of Browning.




 CHAPTER XXXIV.
 THE SECRET MESSAGE

He set the glass down and lay back in his chair, while the waiter
removed the sweet which he had scarcely tasted and set a decanter of
tawny port on the table. He took his snuffbox from his pocket and laid
it on the white cloth before him.

So, after all, what he called the psychological clew, the clew which
is furnished by character and personality rather than by any material
thing, had not failed him, even when he had allowed himself to be
diverted from his path. Appearance had lied about Bob Barling.

It had lied again when it presented Olva Vorloff as a cold-blooded
assassin. The blow she had struck was aimed when she stood in sheer
terror of her life and while she was still suffering from the shock of
horror which the bullet wound must have occasioned.

Those dreadful footsteps in the wood were explanation enough, surely,
of her action in drawing her car across the road and fleeing for
safety to the shelter of the house. At such a moment of anguish any
one who stood in her path would be her enemy.

He rose from the table and prepared to go to bed. Just then the waiter
came back to tell him that Inspector Biles wished to speak to him on
the telephone. He descended to the manager’s office and lifted the
receiver in hands rendered slow by the terrible weariness which again
assailed him.

“Yes. Hailey speaking.”

“There is no news, no news of anybody. Barling has not returned, and
every attempt to find the girl or her accomplices has failed.”

He returned to the sitting room and made a last supreme effort to
conquer his weariness. The haunting conviction that the solution of
the mystery lay almost within his grasp, if only his mind was
quickened to receive it, spurred him to a fresh endeavor.

Should he allow these precious hours, in which perhaps the last hopes
of his friend were being extinguished, to slip fruitlessly by, he
would not afterwards forgive himself. He stooped down and took the
shoes of the dead man; he set them on the table in front of him. Then
he took the broken lace from his pocket and placed it beside them on
the table.

The shoes had been bought in St. James’s Street, of one of the most
renowned makers in London. Their workmanship was of the very highest
order of excellence. He tested the thin soles against the edge of the
table and noted their strength and flexibility. Material of this kind
was costly beyond the purchasing powers of any but the largest purses.
These shoes were quite new and seemed to have been little worn.

He compared their finely-woven laces with the rougher material of the
other lace which, or so he judged, had belonged to footwear of a
vastly inferior type. Even the enameling of the metal points differed
entirely in the two cases.

That on the fine laces was smooth and unscratched, as though the same
meticulous care which had been devoted to the weaving of the fabric
had been devoted also to this minor detail of its efficiency. That on
the broken lace--

He started suddenly and bent down low over the table. He put his hand
up and took his eyeglass, focussing it on the tiny black cylinder.
Then he arose, pushing his chair back so that it fell behind him on
the carpet. He turned to the mantelpiece and searched it for a pin he
had noticed there while examining the contents of Danatoff’s purse the
day before.

He found it behind the purse and at once lifted the broken shoelace
from the table. With the pin point he removed, fragment by fragment,
the minute seal of black wax which, as he had just observed, closed
the extremity of the metal cylinder. When that was done he passed the
pin into the cylinder and then moved it sideways, like a surgeon
probing a dangerous wound.

He withdrew it very slowly.

As he did so there appeared to view the end of a thin strip of tissue
paper which had lain rolled up within and which was inscribed, as he
saw, with many figures.




 CHAPTER XXXV.
 THE LIGHT ON THE CEILING

The tissue paper rolled in his hands, revealing the fact that its
quality was much tougher than its thinness might have suggested. He
spread it out with fingers which shook and gazed on the strange
message which it bore.

He saw a line of numerals, written so closely that he had to focus his
eyeglass on them to read them.

The figures were these:

[Illustration]

They were set down in two continuous lines, each of which contained
forty-one integers. He opened his pocketbook and took from it the copy
he had made of the other cipher which had been discovered in the
lining of Danatoff’s jacket. There could be no doubt that the two were
identical in their character.

They were not, however, identical messages. Indeed the arrangement of
the figures differed completely in the two cases. Those from the
shoelace were not presented in groups of thirteen, as were those from
the jacket, nor, in the former case, had any attempt been made to
indicate such a grouping.

He took paper and pencil and set to work to solve the puzzle his
interest in which was now quickened almost to fever heat. He carried
his writing materials to the armchair beside the fire, because the
night was cold and the room a little chilly. He noticed that the
figures four, seventeen and twenty occurred five times in the course
of the message.

That, in all probability, was the word the. If so, E was represented
by twenty. He counted up and found that there were only seven twenties
in the whole series of figures. It was improbable in the highest
degree that the letter E would occur so seldom--unless, indeed, this
message was written in Russian.

That possibility--or rather probability--came as a damper to his
enthusiasm. Doctor Hailey was a good linguist in a general way, but
Russian was not included in his repertory of foreign tongues. He lay
back, gazing with melancholy eyes at the little figures which seemed
to be executing some complicated dance with the paper before him, as
if mocking his discomfiture.

The dancing of the figures grew slower and slower.

He rose up suddenly and shook himself. He moved to the table and put
out his hand to the pitiless light which had tormented his eyes while
he slept. He switched it out and then returned to his chair. He could
contend no longer against the massed and marshaled forces of Nature.


He awoke slowly, as though his memory called him in a whisper to the
tasks awaiting him. His head was thrown back on the chair; he opened
his eyes and saw only the dim reflection of the fire on the ceiling.

And yet, how cold it was!

A soft sound, the sound of a light footstep on the carpeted floor,
came soothingly to his ear. The reflection of the firelight moved
swiftly away across the dim whitewash.

And then suddenly, as though some vital energy had been discharged
along every nerve of his body, he was completely and acutely awake.

He knew that somebody, somebody whose movements were stealthy and
swift, was in the room. The light that he had seen was the light of an
electric torch.

He moved his head a very little so that his eyes could reach the place
where the light was now shining. As he did so, he strangled on his
lips a cry of sheer amazement.




 CHAPTER XXXVI.
 A LADY IN THE CASE

Standing not five paces from his chair, her face clearly illuminated
by the reflection of the lamp, was the girl whom he had last seen with
Bob Barling at the house in Brunswick Road, Hampstead--the girl who
had accompanied the young man to the Albert Hall and, presumably,
administered to him there the narcotic which had brought about his
collapse and degradation.

He watched her beautiful, pleasure-haunted face with a thrill of new
wonder. She had the loveliness which is so nearly spirituality and yet
which falls so fatally short of that gift.

Her short, well-bred nose, with its infinite provocation, was marred
by the utter weakness, in a moral sense, of her mouth; it was
strangely belied too, by the big mysterious eyes which seemed to
possess a velvety quality in this light. But, over and above these
merely feminine qualities, the face possessed a determination,
actually a hardness, which was unmistakable.

The girl was bending forward over the table, apparently engrossed in
the study of some object lying there. She was dressed, so far as he
could see, in a walking costume and she wore a small hat, from under
the brim of which her golden hair strayed in short curls.

He moved himself very slowly, and with the utmost care, so that he
commanded a clear view of her. Then he saw that she had a pencil in
her hand and was making entries with it in a small notebook. He could
not, from his position, determine whether or not she was copying
another document, but he thought so. His hand moved to the writing
materials on his own lap. They were no longer there.

She ceased writing and stood erect. The lamp lay on the table and her
face was dim toward him. But the shadows which enveloped it only
served to add to its beauty. In a mysterious way they softened the
impression of hardness. She was astonishingly like Olva Vorloff. He
told himself that all women with that type of face are the ready
victims of men such as Tsarov. They possess the psychic temperament on
which the hypnotist produces his most profound impressions.

She stooped and began to gather some objects from the table. He
thought that she was about to leave the room.

With a sudden, instantaneous coordination of all his muscles he sprang
from his chair and was beside her.

The light was extinguished in that instant.

He put out his hand and grasped her. He felt her move swiftly with the
lithe strength of a panther to free herself. His grip tightened. She
shrank down and then recoiled and flung away from him. But he still
held her.

“It is no use,” he said in low tones. “I shall not release you. At the
same time I do not wish--”

He cried out in pain. There was a jingling sound from the fireplace
behind him, and then he felt the girl’s fists raining blows on his
face.

She fought like a tiger-cat with nails bared to wound him. He felt
blood trickling on his cheeks.

But his temper was roused and he abated nothing of his hold on her. It
was obvious that she could not for long cope with a strength so vastly
superior to her own. Indeed, already her efforts seemed to be
flagging.

“Please release me,” she whispered at last, “and I will explain
everything.”

“I can’t trust you.”

“I know; because you saw me last night with Mr. Barling.”

“Precisely.”

“I will tell you something if you release me.” Her voice came in a sob
and he realized that she was on the point of tears.

“You can tell me that now.”

“I am Bob’s friend. My name is Beryl Lestrange. You must have heard
him speak of me.”

“I have never heard him mention your name.”

She sighed deeply. Suddenly a pitiful little laugh escaped her lips.

“Oh, you are holding me tight!” she cried.

Doctor Hailey felt his resolution ebbing. There was a sentimental
trait in his character which he had never been able to master.

“What are you doing here?” he demanded in tones he tried to make
relentless.

“Trying to save the man I love.”

“What!”

“Oh, can’t you understand? Bob is in deadly danger and I am partly
responsible for it. They told me that it was a joke--a wager.”

“I don’t believe you. How can your coming to this room help him in any
way?”

She did not reply for a moment. Then he heard her sobbing. He wondered
what she should do next and was unable to make up his mind.

“I will promise not to try to escape if you will allow me to be free,”
she cried. “You can take me to the door and lock it if you like.”

He considered a moment. Finally he conducted her, limp and
unresisting, to the door of the room. He disengaged one of his hands
and turned the key in the lock; he pulled out the key. Then he
switched up the light and set her at liberty. As he did so she caught
sight of his scarred face and exclaimed in horror.

“Oh, you poor thing. I am so sorry.”

She found her handkerchief and approached him with, he thought,
genuine tenderness in her eyes. He signed to her not to come near.
Already the delicacy of this position was beginning to weigh on his
spirit.

“You realize,” he declared, “that I have no option but to hand you
over to the police. I don’t know why you are here, but I am able to
hazard a guess.”

She sank down on a chair and covered her face with her hands.

“What am I to do!” she cried in helpless bitterness.

“We must wait until the morning.” He drew a deep breath and then
added, “You were sent to steal or copy the cipher found on Danatoff’s
body. Do you deny it?”

She did not raise her head. Its golden hair shone splendidly under the
lamps, reminding him of the _Chateau Yquem_ he had drunk at dinner.
The phrases of an old monkish writer he had read years before came
absurdly to his memory.

“Try to describe the loveliest woman your imagination has ever
approached. Endeavor, afterwards, that shall present to the sense the
perfume of the rose, the fragrance of a bed of thyme; master in a
written page the full flavor of the peach and your prentice hand may
turn to your Bordeaux bins.”

His expression, for the moment, had lost its sternness.

The girl raised her head at last and showed him her face, wet with
tears and fresh as rose petals. Her melancholy eyes were full of
pleading.

“I was compelled, forced, to come here,” she said. “Will you not
believe that?”

“Yes, I will believe that. I will believe it more readily, though, if
you tell me who forced you.”

“The man Tsarov.”

“Who is in love with Olva Vorloff?”

She started. “How do you know?”

“Never mind. It is enough that I know.”

Her eyes had filled with tears again.

“Bob Barling is in love with Olva Vorloff,” she exclaimed bitterly.

She stood up. A smile of triumph gleamed on lips grown suddenly and
strangely insolent.

Doctor Hailey realized that he was looking down the barrel of an
automatic pistol.




 CHAPTER XXXVII.
 AN INSTANT OF PERIL

He contrived to look unconcerned, but he knew that she had
penetrated that mask.

So that was why she had bent over the table in an attitude of such
apparent humility--that she might extract this weapon from a
receptacle which had been out of her reach while he continued to hold
her! Was ever a man befooled so greatly as he had allowed himself to
be befooled?

“I am going to shoot you,” she declared in calm tones, “if you do not
open that door at once and then give me the key.”

Her eyes glowed as she spoke and the melancholy in them was transmuted
to a wild quality with which his professional experience had made him
all too familiar. He realized that, like so many girls of her type and
class Beryl Lestrange had a streak of insanity in her composition. And
she was under the influence and compulsion of Tsarov. He entertained
not the smallest doubt that she meant exactly what she said. His eyes
traveled from her face to the window.

She saw and understood.

“You would not be able to reach it,” she said.

He bowed. In his extremity he felt for his snuffbox and took a large
pinch. He saw curiosity mingle for an instant with the wildness in her
eyes.

“I am not specially anxious to be shot,” he remarked.

“Of course not.” The tones mocked him. “My dear Doctor Hailey, you
must live to bring dozens and dozens more criminals to justice. What
would your poor Inspector Biles do without you?”

Her pink finger moved uneasily on the trigger.

“I am waiting,” she added impudently. He did not move. He was thinking
now of his friend, Bob Barling, and of the fate which, all too
probably, had overtaken that splendid boy. Who was this woman that he
should yield to her? His native courage rejected the idea of
submission no matter what his refusal to submit might cost him. He
resolved and was at peace with himself.

Her finger blanched as she strained on the fatal catch. A queer sense
of unreality stole over him. He recalled the fact that an airman of
his acquaintance had told him once that such a feeling had come to him
when nose-diving, as he supposed to death, over the North Sea during
the war. He remembered the very words which the young fellow had used:
“As soon as I gave up trying to save myself it was quite easy. And oh,
the comfort of it!”

He looked up. The tiny circle of steel gleamed in the lamplight.

“Well?”

The girl’s eyes seemed to lose a little of their steadiness.

“I do not wish to shoot you,” she declared suddenly. “You are a brave
man.”

He shrugged his shoulders and then took a further pinch of snuff, but
his eyes were not removed for an instant from her finger.

“You will open the door?”

“No!”

She fired. He heard the bullet hiss past his ear and go thudding into
the wall behind. Next moment he had dived under the table.

In this position the legs of her chair were just within his reach. He
grasped them and with a tremendous heave of his shoulders swung the
chair upwards, flinging its occupant back towards the fender. The
pistol flew out of her hand and fell some yards away on the skirting
board. He came to it and seized it.

Beryl stumbled to her feet. Her face was terribly pale, and she was
shaking violently.

“Oh, God!” she cried, “I thought I had killed you!”

“Happily not.” His voice was crisp now and his manner decided. Her
fear was transmuted instantly to fury. She glared at him.

And then an amazing thing happened.

With a movement swift as the recoil of a hunted animal she stretched
up her hands and grasped the electric lamp suspended above the table.
She tore it from its bracket and flung it on the floor, where the bulb
exploded noisily. Next instant she had reached the window. She flung
it open and leaped out into the chill darkness of the night.




 CHAPTER XXXVIII.
 A DEAD MAN’S MONEY

Doctor Hailey reached the window just a moment too late. He glanced
out with a sense of horror, which deepened when he perceived that the
yard was so wrapped in shadow as to make any observation impossible.
With fear clutching at his heart he unlocked the door of the room and
sprang out into the corridor. Other people were before him, because
the noise of the pistol shot had brought several visitors from their
beds.

“Quick!” he shouted. “Some one has just fallen from a window.”

He rushed past them to the head of the stair and went crashing down to
the front door. He pulled back the bolt which secured it and tried to
wrench it open. But the stout timbers resisted him. He passed his
hands swiftly up and down the lock, endeavoring to discover the
fastening which had eluded him. One of the other visitors joined him
and struck a match.

The door was secured by an upper bolt running into the lintel above.
He drew it down with a snap.

And then he remembered that the gates of the yard were closed at night
and that, without a key, he could not enter it from the street.

“Do you know where the back door is situated?”

The man at his side did not know. They found the switch and turned on
the lights. A door covered with green baize faced them at the far end
of the hall. The doctor strode to it and threw it open. He passed into
the narrow corridor beyond and came to another door, which opened as
soon as he turned the handle. A faint smell of cooking and the distant
glow of a fire warned him that he had entered the kitchen. He
hesitated, wondering whether to go on or turn back.

And just then he heard behind him the unmistakable sound of a motor
engine being started.

He turned from the kitchen and with outstretched, groping hands made
his way along the corridor. A moment later another door, this time a
heavy structure, like the one at the front of the house, barred his
passage.

He unlocked it and sprang out into the yard.

The sound of the engine was fainter now, as though it had been
throttled down, but he could still hear it. He stood trying to
accustom his eyes to the darkness. Then an exclamation broke from his
lips.

The yard had been suddenly flooded with light.

The light, reflected from the painted doors of the yard, revealed the
outline of his own car. He ran toward it, but as he did so the huge
car lurched forward. He held his breath in spite of himself. At the
same instant, or so it seemed, there was a crash of breaking wood and
metal and the doors of the yard fell away, shattered before the
terrific blow which they had sustained.

The car lurched through the aperture and swung out into the roadway,
its tail lamp, for the moment, casting a blood-red gleam on the newly
accomplished destruction. Then it vanished from sight. Doctor Hailey
rushed after it, but already, when he reached the street, it had left
the hotel far behind.

“My God!”

He turned and saw that others as well as himself had witnessed the
astounding spectacle. They were gathered in a knot at the front door.
He brushed past them and strode to the manager’s office, nearly
knocking down that distressed official on the way.

“Quick,” he urged, “ring up the police at Beaconsfield, at Gerrard’s
Cross, at Uxbridge--everywhere you can think of on the way to London.
No, don’t ask me any questions just now.”

The bewildered man did as he was told.

“What am I to say?” he asked when he had put the first call through.

“Say a thief--a woman--driving a big Daimler car, the headlights of
which are probably injured, is likely to pass going in the direction
of London. Tell them to stop the car at all costs.”

Doctor Hailey waited until his orders had been carried out. Then he
told the manager what had occurred. He added:

“It’s our only chance of catching her. I know the speed of my car,
and, obviously, she knows how to drive it. What I cannot understand is
how she escaped injury. The drop from my window is certainly not less
than fourteen feet--probably it is more.”

“She escaped,” the man declared, “because a flivver, with the hood up,
was standing in the yard just under your window. I had to keep it
there same as I had to keep your Daimler, because the garages are
full. She must have come down straight on to the hood.”

The excellent fellow permitted himself a chastened smile as he
commented:

“I guess she got the pleasantest surprise of her life, don’t you
think?”

“I think so. When did she come to the hotel, in the first instance?”

“Last night by the last train in. She had a dressing case only--said
she was going back to London by the eight-thirty in the morning.” The
manager pointed to the register which lay open on his desk. “There’s
her signature.”

Doctor Hailey adjusted his eyeglass. He read the name Beryl Lestrange.
It was written in a clear, firm hand which bore only the faintest
traces of ornamentation.

The telephone bell rang. The man jumped to the instrument and lifted
the receiver. He listened a moment and then turned to the doctor.

“That’s the Beaconsfield police. No sign of the car yet. They say,
have you rung up Marlowe and Maidenhead, or will they do it
themselves?”

“Tell them to do it themselves. Tell them to take every step they can
think of.”

Doctor Hailey went upstairs and then remembered that the lights in the
room were smashed. He returned to the office and got a candle. He set
it on the table and stood looking round the apartment in which he had
so nearly come to disaster. He sighed, thinking that the enigma of
woman would never be solved.

Girls with golden hair and pink cheeks were the kind of girls he
admired most, and Beryl Lestrange was exactly what his friends called
his “type.” Even now he could scarcely believe that she had
deliberately tried to kill him. Then he told himself that it was
Tsarov, and not the girl, who was responsible. She was in the clutches
of that ruffian, just as Olva Vorloff had been when she struck at
Danatoff.

How strange was the influence of such men over womankind! Again and
again, in his professional life, he had been confronted with cases of
the same sort. Women seemed to have no resistance where the scoundrel
possessed of what they called “psychic” qualities was concerned.

The truth was that women asked of life more than men asked. They
demanded a continuity of emotional experience which it lay beyond the
mental equipment of most husbands and lovers to supply. That was the
explanation of half the bitterness and quarreling in the world. Tsarov
and his kind, on the contrary, had unlimited stocks of excitement and
interest at their disposal.

He moved across the room to the armchair in which he had been sleeping
when the girl entered. As he did so, he caught sight of a piece of
paper lying on the floor. He stooped and picked it up.

It was the strip he had taken from the point of the shoe lace. His
notebook lay beside it and the lace too. But his copy of the cipher
had disappeared.

He returned to the table for the candle and searched round the chair
to make sure that the missing sheet had not slipped away from the
others when they fell from his knees. He moved the candle to and fro
over the surface of the carpet and the fender. The paper was nowhere
to be seen. He was just about to relinquish the search altogether when
a glint of white metal from an object lying in the fender caught his
eye.

He stooped and picked it up. It was a half-crown.

Close beside it lay some other coins. He collected a florin, two
single shillings, three pennies, and a halfpenny. He searched the
hearthrug and found another shilling and a sixpence, and then, on the
skirting board between the fireplace and the window, a penny and a
halfpenny.

He brought these finds to the table and counted them. The total sum
was ten shillings and eleven pence.

Excitement was in his eyes now, because while he was searching, he had
remembered the jingling sound he had heard when he first seized Beryl
Lestrange. She had flung these coins away from her--flung them, as he
now realized, in his face.

Suddenly he cried out.

The sum of ten shillings and eleven pence was the exact amount found
in the purse of the murdered man! He seized his candle again and
turned to the mantelpiece.

Danatoff’s fountain pen was lying where Biles had put it, but his
purse was no longer there.

With hands which were unsteady he set the candle on the table and drew
his notebook to him.




 CHAPTER XXXIX.
 IT IS WRITTEN

He worked for an hour, scarcely lifting his head during the whole
period, except when the manager came to the room to tell him that the
fugitive car had not, so far, been seen by any of the police scouts
sent out to look for it. There was actually impatience in the doctor’s
glance as the good fellow went on to explain that in all probability
the girl had made use of the network of crossroads lying between
Wycombe and Amersham, and so worked her way round to the north of
London.

“It is quite possible.”

The manager went away. Doctor Hailey resumed his labors for a period
and then suddenly rose up and began pacing the room. He took snuff
several times. Once he strode to the window and looked out at the
yard.

“Good Heavens!”

He sprang back to the table and snatched up his pencil. A moment later
a cry broke from his lips.




 CHAPTER XL.
 THE MYSTERIOUS MR. BROWNING

He glanced at his watch. It was six A.M.

He rose and descended with slow steps to the manager’s office. He
found the poor man asleep in his chair, waiting, apparently, for the
telephone to wake him. He put a hand on his shoulder and roused him.

“Can you give me a car to go to London at once?”

“Yes, sir.”

The man shook himself and rose.

“I do badly without my sleep,” he apologized.

“My man can drive it. He will return with it at once. There is no need
to disturb your own people.”

“It’s not a Daimler, sir.”

“That’s all right. I shall take full responsibility.”

Half an hour later Doctor Hailey was on his way to London. His face
retained the grave expression which had come to it before he left the
hotel. Indeed, it seemed to have grown graver as his mind wrestled
with the new problem so suddenly presented to it.

Before the car entered London he took Beryl Lestrange’s pistol from
his pocket and opened it. It contained five unfired cartridges. He put
it back in his pocket again. When he reached Harley Street his usually
dull eyes were glowing.

His butler had breakfast ready and he sat down at once, but not before
he had fetched his copy of _Burke’s Peerage_ from his library. He
opened this volume before he began to eat and read from it at
intervals during the course of his meal. As he read his excitement
increased.

He went to his study and glanced through the morning papers. They
contained columns of news about the “Moorfields Mystery.” The
information that the cipher had been brought to London to be
interpreted was prominently displayed.

Alas! There would be no further necessity now for Biles’s trap. One of
the papers made great capital of “the mysterious Mr. Browning.”

He consulted a time-table. He had scarcely opened it when the
telephone bell buzzed angrily. He lifted the receiver.

“Is that Doctor Hailey’s house?”

“Yes; Doctor Hailey speaking.”

“Your car has been found, sir, on the Spaniards Road, above Hampstead
Heath.”

“Who are you?”

“The Hampstead police. We had a message this morning about it from
Beaconsfield. The car is deserted. The bonnet and wings have been
damaged.”

“Very well, I’ll send my driver up as soon as he returns from Wycombe.
Perhaps you will take charge of it till then.”

He hung up the receiver. He had bigger game to hunt now than Miss
Beryl Lestrange, or even Tsarov. He opened the time-table again. Then
he glanced at his watch. It was after nine o’clock. He rang up the
bootmaker in St. James’s Street whose name was on Danatoff’s shoes. A
moment later he rang the bell.

“Get me a cab, Jenkins,” he ordered.

His butler, who was scarcely less an imposing figure than himself,
bowed and retired. A moment later a cab had come to the curb. He told
the driver to take him to Charing Cross Railway Station.

It was just after eleven o’clock when he emerged from the station at
Maidstone. He engaged a taxicab and gave the address “Maidstone
Abbey.” The cab was an elderly vehicle whose best days, probably, had
been passed on London streets. It emitted strange and sorrowful noises
as it climbed the steep ascent from the roadway. On the high ground,
however, its pace was quite respectable. Doctor Hailey sat well back
on the smooth leather cushions, and once his hand traveled to his
pocket and lingered there a moment or two.

The abbey gates, huge, wrought-metal structures of the kind favored by
the Victorians, were closed. Nor did the lodge-keeper seem to be in
any anxiety to open them. The cabman hooted in vain and at last
descended from his seat and went to reconnoiter. He brought back the
message that the visitor “must give his name, which would then be
telephoned to the house.”

Doctor Hailey hesitated. It was clear that he had not expected this
check.

“Will you say that Mr. Browning is here,” he told the man at last.

He sat fidgeting with his eyeglass as though he half repented of the
step he had taken and was considering alternative plans of action. The
cabman went to the door of the lodge to await the answer to the
message. A few minutes elapsed and then the lodge-keeper emerged and
began to open the gates. A shade of astonishment crossed the doctor’s
face.

The avenue was richly wooded with splendid oaks and beeches, such as
are only seen in South County English parks. Its generous curves
disclosed new beauties in a succession that, for all its swiftness,
was never bewildering. Delicious vistas of pasture lands, stretching
to warm horizons, greeted the visitor’s eye, creating an illusion of
summer even under the winter sky.

And then the avenue broke from the woodland and dipped down to an old
bridge with high stone parapets which moss and lichen had transfigured
from their native gray to a mosaic of green and pale gold. Across the
bridge and rising, as it seemed, from the banks of the little stream
which ran before its face, was the abbey itself, one of the most
glorious of Tudor remains.

It was built of red brick, “hallowed,” in apt phrase, by the centuries
and rendered fairer by the small-leafed ivy which strayed over it. The
doctor caught his breath, for the sunlight lay mellow on the
bewitching facade and sparkled on the surface of the water below it.
The sheer delight of the spectacle banished, for the moment, even the
dark forebodings which had pursued him all the way from London.

The cab swung across the bridge and mounted the incline leading to the
front door.

Doctor Hailey descended and told the man to wait for him. He rang the
great bell which, in spite of its size, moved easily. A footman in
livery opened the door.

“Is Lord Borrodean at home?”

The man stood back to allow him to enter. He found himself in a vast
hall, the stained glass windows of which emitted a gentle light which
made striking contrast with the golden sunshine he had just quitted.
The silence of the years seemed to dwell under this dim room, beneath
which tattered flags and old weapons hung in profusion as though
history had left something of her material self to decorate this
mansion.

“His lordship will see you in the library.”




 CHAPTER XLI.
 BORRODEAN HEARS SOME NEWS

The footman took Doctor Hailey’s hat and gloves and then conducted
him across the wide parquet floor, with its great skins and deep rugs,
to a door situated near the main staircase of the house. He opened it
and the doctor found himself once again in the sunlight, with the
winsome meadows stretching before his eyes.

He glanced round at the room and restrained a sigh of wonder. There
was, he thought, nothing in the whole world to compare with these old
English county houses. The richness of their beauty seemed to defy
description, even as it defied all analysis.

They belonged to the soil of England as truly as her villages and her
gardens and her solemn woodlands with their comfortable trees. Indeed,
they expressed her spirit--that slow, stern faith which builds in
defiance of time and season, leaving always to Nature herself the task
of completing and mellowing the structure.

The oak paneling was wonderful beyond even his expectation, perfection
of workmanship being wedded in every inch of its glorious surface to
that ripe beauty which neither craftsmanship nor care can bestow. The
great open fireplace with its massive iron “dogs.”

He heard a step behind him and turned sharply.

An oldish man, with a face lined with heavy sorrow, stood before him.
He carried his left arm in a sling.

“Lord Borrodean?”

“Yes, sir.”

The owner of Maidstone Abbey suggested in a striking manner his rich
inheritance. He possessed a face which all women called “beautiful”--a
cold face, yet with the living traces of tenderness and sympathy. His
gray eyes were unflinching in their truth. As he looked into them
Doctor Hailey felt himself gasp with horror. Then his eyes traveled to
Lord Borrodean’s footwear and the sense of horror was increased.

“The name which I gave a few minutes ago to your lodge-keeper,” he
said, “is not my own name--”

He broke off. Lord Borrodean’s face had become ashy pale. He seemed to
totter and reached out his hand to the back of a chair to steady
himself. Doctor Hailey took a step forward, but the old man waved him
back with a gesture so imperious that he desisted instantly.

“What is your name?” he asked in frigid tones.

“Hailey--Eustace Hailey.”

“What! The Doctor?”

“Yes.”

The silence in the great room became oppressive. Lord Borrodean had
recovered himself completely and seemed to be measuring his antagonist
with his cold eyes as, doubtless, his ancestors had measured the men
they fought and discomfited in earlier days.

“Why have you come here?” he demanded at last.

“I have come to visit Mr. Browning.”

Again the pale cheeks grew paler. Again the old man seemed to sway on
his feet. But once more the tough spirit which animated him came to
his support.

“I do not understand you,” he declared in measured tones.

“In that case,” Doctor Hailey said, “I shall beg leave to enlighten
you, for the matter cannot be delayed. Let me explain at once that my
friend, Inspector Biles of Scotland Yard, has invited my assistance in
the affair which is now, I believe, spoken of as ‘The Moorfields
Mystery’--the affair of the murder of a Russian named Danatoff.”

“Stop!” Lord Borrodean raised a minatory hand. “I say at once that I
refuse to discuss that horrible business with anybody. Moorfields is
my property, but that fact does not entitle the police to molest me,
as they have recently attempted to do.”

He turned to the door. The interview so far as he was concerned, was
at an end. Doctor Hailey stood his ground.

“Possibly not,” he remarked in cool tones. “But the matter wears a
somewhat different complexion, I think, when it can be shown that you
yourself were present at the tragedy which the police are now
investigating.”

His eyes, as he spoke, were fixed sternly on the old man’s face. Lord
Borrodean wilted visibly.

“You do not deny it?”

“Forgive me, I have not been very well lately. I cannot see you
further.”

The doctor bit his lip.

“Lord Borrodean,” he said in quiet tones, “I must urge you, beg you,
not to adopt this attitude. Believe me the matter is far too serious
to be dismissed in any cavalier fashion. The evidence that you are
concerned, deeply implicated would be a fitter description, in the
Moorfields tragedy, is, I think, conclusive. You cannot, believe me,
hope to escape the inevitable consequences of this discovery.”

He paused. The old man had sunk into a chair while he was speaking. He
sat now looking before him with strangely tortured eyes.

“What do you want of me?” he said in tones so low that the doctor
could scarcely hear them.

“I want you to tell me exactly what happened on the fatal night.”

Lord Borrodean did not seem to hear that adjuration. His face remained
almost expressionless.

“It is quite true,” he said, “I was at Moorfields on the night of the
murder.”




 CHAPTER XLII.
 WHAT HAILEY KNEW

He motioned Doctor Hailey to a chair and remained silent until he
had seated himself.

“I have not spoken,” he declared, “for two reasons, the validity of
which I leave to your judgment. The first is, that I am unable to
throw any light on the crime, and the second that if my presence at
the house were known terrible disaster might befall innocent men and
women whose safety is at present in my keeping.”

He turned his head to the doctor as he said this, challenging him with
his eyes. Doctor Hailey frowned slightly.

“I fear that does not carry us very far,” he remarked in cold tones.

“I can tell you no more.”

“My dear sir, imagine yourself, for a moment, in the witness box.”

“No, no. It shall not be!” The old man’s tones rang out so that his
hearer was startled. His eyes flashed and a deep flush of scarlet
mounted to his brow.

“It is inevitable.”

The surge of anger spent itself. Lord Borrodean bowed his head.

“If you choose to ask me any questions you may have framed in your
mind,” he said, “I will answer them so far as I can do so without
danger to others.”

Doctor Hailey took his notebook from his pocket.

“It is recorded in _Burke’s Peerage_,” he declared, “that your son,
the Hon. Michael Borrodean, married in nineteen hundred and thirteen
while Military Attaché at the British Embassy in St. Petersburg, the
Countess Tatiana Ivanovna, daughter of a Russian nobleman named
Sergius. That is correct?”

“It is correct. I furnished that information myself.”

“Your son came to England at the outbreak of war and rejoined the
Coldstream Guards. He fought through the war and then joined the
Wrangel Expedition against the Bolshevists?”

The old man bowed. “Just so.”

“In that expedition he lost his life?”

“‘Wounded and missing’ was the official description at first. It was
not for many months that I received definite assurance of his death.”

Lord Borrodean’s voice broke as he said these words, but he recovered
himself immediately.

“So that you have very special and personal reasons for disliking the
present rulers of the Russian Empire?”

“That is manifest.”

Doctor Hailey’s voice became more gentle.

“To some extent at any rate,” he queried, “the same is true of the
Countess Olva?”

“Yes; her father was murdered.”

Lord Borrodean’s eyes became rather blank as he made this statement.

“The murdered man, Danatoff, was known to you?”

“He was known to me.”

“And to the Countess Olva?”

“Oh, yes.”

“And you had arranged to meet him at Moorfields on Monday night?”

The old man bowed his head.

“Yes.”

He spoke the monosyllable sharply.

Doctor Hailey resolved to play at once the strongest card in his hand.
He took his pocketbook from his pocket and extracted from it the
broken shoe lace he had found at Moorfields. He handed it, without
comment, to his companion.

The effect was more startling even than he had anticipated. Indeed for
a moment he feared that Lord Borrodean had fainted, so still and
lifeless did his face become.

“Where did you find this?” he asked in a hoarse whisper.

“At Moorfields, in the yard.”

Doctor Hailey raised his head and looked the old man squarely in the
eyes.

“I am aware,” he stated, “of the secret connected with it. I have
extracted the paper and read its message.” He added: “That is the
reason why I have come here this morning.”

Lord Borrodean closed his eyes. His body seemed to sway slightly, as
though he were recovering from some blow which had nearly vanquished
him.

“What is the message?” he asked in tones the anxiety of which he did
not even attempt to hide.

“This: ‘Very urgent, or may denounce Borrodean.’”

“Oh, my God!”

Again the terrible silence fell in the room. The soft notes of a clock
floated on the sunny air. Suddenly the old man leaned forward toward
his visitor.

“The shoes from which this lace was torn are in the possession of the
police, I take it?” he asked.

Doctor Hailey hesitated. They had reached the crisis which it had been
his object from the beginning to precipitate. When he spoke his tones
were slow and deliberate:

“They are not in possession of the police,” he said. “They were
removed from the dead man’s body and replaced by another pair of
shoes.”

“What!”

Lord Borrodean started from his chair. Fear of the most deadly kind
was imprinted on his features. The doctor eyed him with cool
hostility.

“I should have imagined,” he observed, “that by this time you must
have realized the futility of further concealment.”

His mouth hardened as he spoke, but his companion’s expression did not
change.

“What do you mean?” he asked in staccato accents.

“I mean that I have reason to suppose--good reason--that the shoes
which Danatoff was wearing when his body was found by the police
belonged to yourself.”

Lord Borrodean’s face lost its pallor in an instant. His eyes blazed.

“You fool!” he cried. He repeated the words in accents of
extraordinary bitterness.

Doctor Hailey had waited until the storm had spent itself, but his
composure was visibly less than it had been. He was forced to
acknowledge that this was by no means the reception he had anticipated
for his accusation.

“The shoes were made by your bootmaker,” he declared; “they resemble
very closely those you are now wearing. By no conceivable possibility
could they belong to any of the other actors in the crime.”

He paused a moment and then added: “You will scarcely deny that the
car registered in the name of Browning is your car, or that the flat
leased by Browning in Jermyn Street is your flat; or that you paid a
visit, surreptitiously, to Danatoff’s room in the High Court Hotel?
Moreover, do not forget that the message of which the murdered man was
the bearer--”

He stopped suddenly. To his astonishment he saw that Lord Borrodean
was not even listening to him.




 PART IV




 CHAPTER XLIII.
 LORD BORRODEAN SPEAKS

Doctor Hailey counted that moment among the most humiliating of his
life. He had come to Maidstone Abbey to extract a confession which, he
believed, would be the means of rescuing Bob Barling from terrible
danger. He had taken this course, too, without consulting Biles,
because he foresaw that the latter’s hesitation to arrest a man in
Lord Borrodean’s position without evidence of the most conclusive
kind, must bring about prolonged and possibly fatal delay. And he had
failed.

He did not doubt his failure from the moment at which he perceived
that his accusations had lost interest for the individual against whom
they were leveled. That, perhaps, is the supreme proof of innocence.
Bewilderment and self-reproach laid hold of his spirit.

He wondered whether or not he should go away at once and leave to the
police the task of probing further Lord Borrodean’s connection with
the mystery. However deeply they probed, it was certain that they
would not find the evidence of guilt which, in his blindness, he had
supposed must be immediately forthcoming.

Lord Borrodean’s voice roused him from this bitter reflection.

“I have made up my mind,” the old man declared in sorrowful tones,
“that I cannot longer keep to myself the terrible secret which
oppresses me. So far as I can see, in face of this overwhelming
catastrophe, all promises are void.”

He was speaking more to himself than to his visitor. His eyes had a
far-away look and the expression of anxiety on his face was
intensified. He came to where the doctor was sitting and stood before
him.

“What I am about to tell you,” he said, “will place the safety of many
people in your hands. I can only pray God that you will use the
knowledge with discretion.”

His features were quite calm now and his bearing had resumed its
native dignity. He remained a few seconds and then returned to his own
chair. He leaned forward grasping one of the arms of it with his
uninjured hand.

“The fact is,” he declared, “that my son Michael was not killed. His
great knowledge of the Russian language and of Russian customs and
ways of life saved him. He escaped from this prison and at the present
moment is living in disguise in Petersburg, conducting a wide
organization, the object of which is to snatch as many as possible of
their victims from the hands of the Bolshevists. He has some
employment in connection with the National Theatre.”

He paused a moment; then he added:

“Happily, there is almost no limit to the cupidity of some of these
people. Provided the price can be paid, salvation is procurable in a
considerable number of instances.”

His hands moved restlessly, opening and closing on the massive wooden
arm of the chair.

“I should explain, perhaps,” he continued, “that my son’s dear wife
was one of the earliest victims of the Revolution. They killed her as
they killed so many others, in secret, regardless of the fact that, as
the wife of an Englishman, she was no longer one of their own people.

“You will understand now, I think, why he has undertaken this work and
why he is pursuing it with the most complete contempt for its dangers.
I need not tell you that if his identity were suspected for a single
moment, he would pay instant forfeit with his life.”

He paused again. Doctor Hailey inclined his head.

“There is no doubt on that score,” he said.

Lord Borrodean passed his hand across his brow in a gesture of
infinite weariness.

“I have been acting during the past two years,” he said, “as the chief
organizer of the work in this country, and because of the terrible
danger threatening my boy and his friends in Russia, I have left no
stone unturned to secure and maintain the most absolute secrecy in
regard to all my actions. No precaution I can say honestly, which
human wit has been able to suggest, has ever been neglected.”

He rose and began to pace the room with restless steps.

“I made up my mind, in the first instance, as soon as I got into
communication with my boy that I must have help in carrying on the
work. My own knowledge of Russia and Russian affairs is but trifling.
After long consideration, I decided, for reasons with which I need not
trouble you just now, to approach the Countess Olva Vorloff-- She is
my son’s late wife’s first cousin.”

There was a strange huskiness in his voice as he pronounced that name.
He seemed, too, to pause for a moment and then to increase the
rapidity of his speaking.

“I made her my confidant in everything--she only. The others have
enjoyed at most but half-knowledge. That was a part of my system of
defense against the espionage of which, sooner or later, we were bound
to become the object. But I met her very seldom and never in
public--never even in London.

“It was arranged that all conferences should take place in the garage
at Moorfields and that we should each travel there separately in our
own cars very late at night. Moreover, the cars used for this purpose
were registered under assumed names and kept in public garages. There
were, of course, flaws even in that scheme, but it served us well
until this fearful trouble occurred.”




 CHAPTER XLIV.
 MARKED FOR A VICTIM

He paused in his pacing of the floor and swung round toward the
doctor:

“Our agent, Danatoff, always met us at Moorfields,” he declared. “He
retained a room permanently at the High Court Hotel and was known
there as a merchant with big interests in Poland. He traveled between
London and Warsaw at frequent intervals and he had rooms also in one
of the principal hotels in that city.

“He had orders, when he reached London, to walk up Bond Street between
noon and half past twelve o’clock and on the days his return might be
expected the Countess Olva always visited that street. They met, thus,
quite openly and casually. Sometimes they spoke to one another, but,
more often merely bowed. But it was arranged that a meeting at
Moorfields should always take place on the night following these
encounters.

“The Countess used to summon me by telephone and invariably employed a
public call office for this purpose. We had, further, an arrangement
in which Danatoff shared, that if we happened to meet at any time and
did not wish to recognize each other, we should glance down for a
moment before passing. These, Doctor Hailey, are only a few of the
precautions we took for the preservation of my beloved boy.”

Lord Borrodean resumed his seat and covered his face with his hand.
The doctor watched him with a growing sense of uneasiness. This
disclosure explained much; but it raised new doubts and new anxieties.
A feeling that he dared not wait here much longer, while the sands, in
all probability, were running out swiftly elsewhere for Bob and
perhaps for Olva, too, caused him to shift uneasily in his chair. The
old man glanced up in quick comprehension.

“I am sorry to detain you so long,” he apologized, “but it seemed
necessary to make this explanation in view of what has occurred since.
The rest is soon told. On Monday last at one o’clock in the afternoon,
or thereabouts, I received the message to come to Moorfields.

“I arrived there, I think, about three A.M., and drove up the main
carriageway to the house. I had only my side lamps on, as I always
avoided the use of headlights for fear of attracting attention, but
nevertheless, I got a clear view of the yard as I entered it--”

His voice fell to a whisper and it seemed to the doctor that he
shuddered.

“_The yard was quite empty of vehicles. Yet Olva Vorloff was standing
there with blood streaming down her face, and a pistol in her hand.
The moment that she saw my car appear, she raised the weapon and began
to fire at me._”

He sprang up again, as the terrible recollection flooded his mind.

“At first,” he cried, in tones suddenly grown strident, “I scarcely
realized what had occurred; but the crash of the breaking glass and
the screech of the bullets past my ears soon brought enlightenment. I
flung the door open and threw myself out into the shadows which
surrounded the vehicle. And just then a strange thing happened. The
girl seemed to totter forward. She fell full length on the tiles of
the yard and the pistol fell out of her hand with a clatter.

“I jumped to her side and saw that she had fainted. There was a big
wound on the side of her head and she was bleeding freely. I tried to
lift her up, when suddenly another bullet came whizzing past my ear. I
glanced up and saw a man standing at the edge of the shrubbery with a
pistol in his hand.

“I distinctly saw the gleam of the barrel in the moonlight. I
realized, then, the nature of the trap into which I had been lured. My
only thought was for my son. I relinquished the girl and made a dash
for the avenue, and as I did so, he shot at me again. The bullet
struck my left shoulder, just above the joint.

“He fired several times, but failed to inflict any other wounds, nor
did he pursue me further than the front of the house. Happily, when I
reached the main road a market wagon was passing on its way to London.
A few minutes after I had entered it, a big car, traveling at a
tremendous speed, passed us going in the same direction.

“I can say honestly that I scarcely noticed the pain of my wound
during the terrible hours which followed. My one anxiety was to assure
myself that the man who had attempted to assassinate me was not
Danatoff--for in that case I realized that all hope for my dear son
must be abandoned.

“I left the wagoners at the High Court Hotel and entered that
building. A very sleepy night porter answered my summons and, I fancy,
took me for some late reveler returning to his room. At any rate, he
asked no questions. I mounted the stair and went straight to
Danatoff’s bedroom. To my horror, the door was standing ajar. He had
gone-- At that moment, a kind of frenzy overcame me, because I
searched the room from the floor to ceiling.”

Lord Borrodean’s voice broke as he said this, and Doctor Hailey saw
that his eyes were moist. But his proud lips did not falter.

“I closed the bedroom door and left the hotel soon after that,” he
concluded, “and drove to the garage where Danatoff kept his car. The
car had not been taken out. Then I drove to Jermyn Street and--let me
confess it--gave myself up to despair.”




 CHAPTER XLV.
 SHARING A SECRET

Doctor Hailey had listened to this recital in silence; and, even
now, when it was finished, he found himself so completely at a loss to
understand it that he would gladly have postponed further discussion
until he had had an opportunity of collecting his thoughts. But
already the chill of early afternoon was in the air. He rose to his
feet and walked to the window, gazing vacantly over the meadows.

“You are quite clear in your own mind,” he asked after a few minutes,
“that the Countess Olva was awaiting your coming?”

“I have not the slightest doubt on that score. She stood facing the
avenue, with her weapon ready for instant use.”

The doctor turned and remained with his back to the window.

“It is just possible, however, is it not, that she may have expected
another car with another occupant?”

“The other car would have been Danatoff’s and, as you know, she had
already succeeded in killing him. God knows, I have tried to resist
it, but the conclusion is inevitable that she has played the traitor.
That shoelace is the final, most convincing proof of all.”

Doctor Hailey took a pinch of snuff.

“There,” he said, “I venture to disagree with you. If the murderer had
known the significance of the shoelace, he would scarcely have left
the broken end of it lying in the yard.”

He spoke very deliberately, but Lord Borrodean was not convinced.

“That,” he declared, “might apply to a person acting calmly. But this
girl was laboring under fearful excitement. Even during the few
moments I was able to observe her, I saw that her eyes were wild and
her features distorted.” He broke off, and then added:

“Moreover, the fact that you have been able to read the cipher proves
that some one in possession of that secret must have unwittingly
afforded you a clew to it. Even Danatoff was ignorant of its exact
nature and no one, so far as I know, has ever been able to discover it
unaided. Olva Vorloff invented the cipher. She alone shared the secret
with me.”




 CHAPTER XLVI.
 WHO KILLED DANATOFF?

Before he left Maidstone Abbey, Doctor Hailey did what he could to
ease Lord Borrodean’s anxieties. He felt great pity for the old man
whose love for his son had become something nearly approaching a
mania. It was terrible to think that all the elaborate precautions he
had taken had been unavailing.

He gave him an account of the progress so far made in the elucidation
of the case, and supplied, in so doing, a few rags of evidence tending
to the conclusion that the secret of the cipher might still remain
inviolate. That hope was strengthened somewhat by the fact that Lord
Borrodean’s precautions had not been confined to England, but had
extended also to Warsaw.

As he drove away from the Abbey, however, the doctor experienced a
sharp reaction from this rather forced optimism. So far as he could
see, no real progress of any kind had been achieved. On the contrary,
the investigation had come to one of those blank walls which are the
bane of the detective’s life. With danger increasing every hour and
the possible sources of information becoming fewer and progressively
less hopeful, the outlook was black indeed.

He had an empty compartment in the train and lay back with closed eyes
in the attitude which experience had taught him was most favorable to
the accurate working of his mind. Once again, he tried to reconstruct
the scene of the murder, in the light of fresh evidence. There could
be no doubt now that the car in which Lord Borrodean reached
Moorfields--the car in which Danatoff’s body was found--had brought no
other actor to the scene of the tragedy. Any idea that Tsarov had
traveled in it might be dismissed once for all.

How then had Tsarov traveled? How had Danatoff traveled? The
investigations carried out by Biles and himself had been quite
thorough enough to make it practically certain that only three cars
took part in the night’s work--Lord Borrodean’s car, the Hillman
coupé which belonged to Olva Vorloff, and the car which at one period
or another, had been in collision with the Hillman coupé.

Danatoff had undoubtedly traveled in one or the other of those two
vehicles, because the hour of the murdered man’s visit to South Street
made it absolutely certain that he had not reached Wycombe by train.

He opened his eyes suddenly and sat erect. Could it be that Danatoff
had traveled in the Hillman coupé with Olva? She had left a message
